<meta>This page continues the fragmentary study of the "Tria Prima"—Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury—focusing on how these three principles interact to change the physical state of a substance, specifically regarding its stability (fixation) or its ability to evaporate (volatility).</meta>

By means of Salt, Sulfur is in a state of flow <note>original: "fluore"; referring to a liquid or melted state</note> in moisture.
By means of Sulfur, <unclear>however</unclear>, <unclear>it in every way</unclear> <unclear>becomes</unclear> <term>fixed</term> <note>original: "fixum"; in alchemy, a fixed substance is one that remains stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to high heat.</note>.
By means of Sulfur, Salt does not dissolve in moisture.
But by means of Mercury, Salt and Sulfur become <term>volatile</term> <note>original: "volatilia"; substances that easily transform into a gaseous state or evaporate.</note>.

<meta>The previous page concluded a technical discussion on the three alchemical principles—Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury—specifically how their interactions govern the physical properties of substances. This page is the formal title page for a major supplementary work by the same author, Johann Rudolph Glauber, intended to clarify and complete his entire literary corpus.</meta>

# Johann Rudolph Glauber’s
# AN ARK OPULENT WITH TREASURES,

->OR<-

## A GENERAL APPENDIX

->*To all the books published*<-
->*thus far,*<-

Which not only illuminates all the obscure passages in Philosophical and Medical as well as Chemical writings, and explains those things difficult to understand, but also so abundantly supplies what is lacking, that the Learned and Unlearned, the Highest and the Lowest, indeed even the lowliest Artisans and Farmers may be more than able to comprehend that in all his writings, Glauber has written the pure, unadulterated truth; and has brought forth into the light once again the most noble art of Alchemy <note>original: "Alchymiæ"; in the 17th century, the boundary between chemistry and alchemy was fluid, often referred to as "Chymistry."</note>, which until now has lain so long in the darkness, and has revealed it for the common good of the Human Race.

---

To the honor of the Eternal, Incomprehensible, Omnipotent, and Just God, Father and Origin of all good, for the aid and defense of sacred and oppressed truth, and for the benefit of the whole Human Race; a work written by the Author himself, with Divine Aid, and not without sweat, expense, and labor, and distributed into Ten Centuries <note>original: "Centurias"; a "century" in this context refers to a book or section containing one hundred distinct observations, recipes, or paragraphs.</note>.

---

<summary>This is the title page for Johann Rudolph Glauber's "General Appendix," a 1660 publication intended to serve as a comprehensive key to all his previous works on alchemy, medicine, and philosophy. It emphasizes the accessibility of his "unadulterated truth" to all people, from scholars to common laborers.</summary>

<meta>This page begins the author's preface to the reader, establishing a continuity of intent from his previous publications. He addresses delays in releasing several promised alchemical treatises due to personal and professional obstacles.</meta>

# PREFACE.

## Kind Reader.

It is a common proverb that every promise becomes a debt, and therefore, standing by one's word is an act that brings great honor to the young and the old alike. Since I have pledged my reputation in my previous writings by promising several small works, and yet until now have been unable to satisfy the expectations and desires of many by publishing them—due to a lack of time, and indeed, because greater inconveniences and obstacles hinder me from day to day from writing more—I wish for nothing more than to fulfill my word by standing by my promises and publishing the said works. 

These include, namely, my *Vegetable Work*, the *Work of Saturn* <note>In alchemy, Saturn is the traditional name for the metal lead.</note>, the *Chemical Dialogue*, the *Fourth Part of the Spagyric Pharmacopoeia* <note>The term "Spagyric" refers to alchemical medicine, which involves separating and then recombining the essential components of a substance to create a more potent remedy.</note>, and that wonderful little book on the *Concentration of Heaven and Earth*. These are treatises that truly contain the most excellent secrets <note>original: "arcana"; referring to hidden or profound mysteries of nature.</note> and are highly useful to the human race, being most worthy for men to possess. However, at a time—

<meta>catchword: whom-</meta>

<summary>The author apologizes to his readers for the delay in publishing several promised alchemical and medical treatises, listing titles such as the 'Work of Saturn' and the 'Spagyric Pharmacopoeia.' He emphasizes his desire to fulfill his professional debt despite ongoing personal obstacles.</summary>
<keywords>preface, alchemy, Spagyric medicine, Saturn, arcana, publication delay, Vegetable Work, concentration of heaven and earth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's apology for the delay in publishing his promised works. He explains that instead of individual volumes, he is combining his findings into a single "General Appendix" to save time and ensure the information reaches the public. This page transitions from his excuses to a description of the current volume's structure.</meta>

# PREFACE.

—when I am necessarily deprived of the ability to write each treatise individually. For this reason, I am compelled to follow a shorter path and to publish the aforementioned treatises with less labor and difficulty for the sake of the public good. This present Treatise fulfills this purpose, titled ***The Universal Ark Abounding in Riches, or a General Appendix*** <note>original: "Universalis Arcæ divitiis opulentæ, sive Appendicis Generalis"</note> to all my writings published thus far. In this work, everything that was previously stated too briefly or unclearly is explained with greater clarity, and things entirely omitted are supplied. At the same time, the promised treatises are included—not, however, in the order they would otherwise have been written (as maintaining that order here would cause great difficulty), but in the sequence in which these <term>Secrets</term> <note>The author uses "Secreta" to refer to *arcana*, the hidden operations of nature and chemistry discovered through experimental practice.</note> became known to me over time and were committed to paper.

It remains the choice of any reader, at their own discretion, to organize ***Medical Secrets with Medical matters, Minerals with Minerals, and Chemical things with Chemicals***, should it please them and should time allow—a luxury that is by no means granted to me. Let anyone who is satisfied and content with these offerings consider this: if a Cook, short on time, were to serve a platter filled with the finest foods,

# PREFACE.

—in which it was not possible for him to place each individual dish upon its own separate platter; could anyone, therefore, rightly be angry with him, or dare to grumble <note>The original Latin *ogganniendo* suggests a snarling or growling complaint, like that of a dog.</note> that the ignorance of a **Cook** unskilled in culinary matters must be blamed for unskillfully throwing together so many delicate and grand foods into a single dish and daring to serve them? I do not believe anyone could or would rightly complain about such a deed by any Cook. The **Cook**, wishing to be helpful, served such foods as he had; he who refuses to take them may exercise his own freedom and pass over those things he is not forced to accept, just as the **Cook** cannot be forced by anyone to prepare them except by his own judgment. Whatever food does not please one person's palate will not be distasteful to another's appetite, but on the contrary, will be welcome, since one food usually tastes good to this man, and another to that; nor is he affected by disgust at the disorderly mixture of foods, who picks out what is tasty for himself and leaves the rest for others.

Let everyone who obtains these ***Writings*** do the same—not by taking them in a bad light, but with courtesy and by kindly excusing the fact that they

# PREFACE.

*...I have not released them in a more polished order. These writings are like a true and great chest or ark, filled with a vast number of excellent secrets <term>arcana</term> <note>The author uses both "arcana" and "secreta," referring to hidden knowledge or specialized recipes not known to the general public.</note>, which I have gathered into my own knowledge through successive investigation <term>indagatione</term> over thirty years. They have been gathered into a single heap so that they might either be kept for my own use or, at some point, be made public property. From this ark, anyone will be able to draw out those mysteries and secrets that appeal to them or serve their particular needs.*

*As far as I am concerned, since I see every day now this person, now that one, snatched away by death and committed to the earth, I can easily reckon that my own turn will come in a short time. I would be very loath to allow so many secrets—acquired with such great expense, labor, and hardship—to be wasted by taking them with me into the grave <note>literally "under the earth"</note> rather than sharing them for the common good. In this, I shall perform the duty of a good father of a household <term>Patris familiâs</term> or a skilled steward <term>Oeconomus</term> <note>In the early modern period, the Oeconomus was the manager of a large estate's resources.</note>. Such a man, after he has provided abundantly for himself, his wife, his children, and his whole family with winter <term>hyberno</term> clothing, does not throw away any surplus of linen <term>pannorum lineorum</term> or woolen <term>laneorum</term> cloths. Instead, he places them in a chest to be kept until he finds an opportunity to put them to use for his neighbor...*

Thus, when in the winter time he perceives the imprudent cicadas <note>The author uses the classical fable of the Grasshopper (cicada) and the Ant to illustrate lack of foresight.</note>—who, throughout the entire summer season, did not imitate the diligence of the busy bees and ants, but instead spent that most noble time uselessly in idling, singing, and dancing—wandering about with naked bodies, starving stomachs, and thirsty throats, he opens his storehouse chest, which he has filled for such necessities. By doing so, he makes those in need participants in the abundance available to him, so that they may be able to survive and overcome the most troublesome winter.

I have not sent these works forth in a more polished order. They are like a true and great chest or ark, filled with very many excellent secrets <term>secrets (Latin: secreta)</term> <note>In this context, "secrets" refers to specialized knowledge or hidden techniques in science and medicine, rather than something meant to be kept hidden from everyone.</note>, which were brought into my knowledge through successive investigation over thirty years, and heaped together into one pile so that they might either be preserved for my own use or eventually be made a matter of public right <note>original: "publici juris"; meaning to be published for the common good.</note>. From this ark, anyone will be able to draw out those mysteries and secrets which please them or serve their particular needs.

As far as I am concerned, since I see daily now this man, now that one, snatched away by death, carried out and committed to the earth, I can easily reckon my own accounts and realize that those same turns of fate will reach me in a short time. I would very reluctantly allow so many secrets—obtained at such great expense, labor, and trouble—to be taken under the earth with me in vain, rather than contributing them to the common good. I shall perform here the duty of a good father of the family <term>father of the family (Latin: Pater familiâs)</term>, or a skilled steward <term>steward (Latin: Oeconomus)</term>, who, after he has abundantly provided winter clothing <note>original: "vestitu hyberno"</note> for himself, his wife, his children, and his whole household, if he still has an abundance of linen and woolen cloths remaining, he does not throw them away. Instead, he casts them into a chest to be guarded until he has found an opportunity to apply them to the use of his neighbor...

# PREFACE

<column-break/>

...the body, raging like a plague, fire, famine, snow, and hail, and afflicting all things. May the Divine hand mercifully turn this away from us, yet because of our sins, it is unlikely to be withheld; it shall suddenly overwhelm mortals who are secure and heedless. And because a series of so many years has cheered us with summer serenity, and has not afflicted us with any troublesome storms of winter time, I seem to foresee by my own conjecture that if once the hardships and afflictions of the time overwhelm us, they will not cease so quickly, but will drag on with a longer delay. For that reason, so that I might bring comfort and aid to the needy, I have been led in my mind to bring forth from my open storehouse many excellent and useful arts <note>The author uses "arts" here to mean practical skills, crafts, or technical secrets.</note>, whereby every person, if only they are willing, may abundantly provide for themselves and their own, and be able to spend their life much more comfortably in the coming winter time than the greedy man, even with the benefit of his most ample wealth. For such riches can provide him very little help when he is struggling in such hardships of the times, whereas on the contrary, <term>Art</term> <note>original: "Ars"; referring to specialized knowledge or skill</note> is accustomed at all times and in all places to rescue its possessor from hardships and difficulties of this kind. To no better use could I put this most rich <term>Treasure</term> <note>original: "Thesaurus"; a common title for books containing a collection of knowledge</note>, gathered with the greatest labors...

# PREFACE

<margin>A 5</margin>

*...I could have preserved it, than to consecrate it to the honor of the Almighty God, the giver of all good things from whom I received it, and to the comfort, help, and health of the needy. For whatever good is bestowed upon men suffering from want in this fellowship of life—even if it be but the smallest drink of water—God will compensate with a thousandfold reward in the next world, as is evident from the 25th chapter of Matthew <note>A reference to the biblical parable of the sheep and the goats, where charity to the "least of these" is seen as service to God.</note>. To what better use, therefore, could I expend my collected treasures than this? Since there is no comparison in the exchange if temporal things are traded for eternal ones; for the former are fleeting and perish in a moment, while the latter are perpetual and never pass away. Should I, after the manner of greedy men, guard these treasures for my children alone? I am by no means certain whether they will live long enough to be able to put them to use. I say nothing of thieves, fire, and water, by which such things can be stolen or ruined. In this way, however—namely, by publishing my writings for the common good and benefit of the Human Race—I not only manifest and amplify the Divine miracles and the honor of God himself, but also, through this work, the miser-*

<meta>catchword: -cordia (mercy)</meta>

I do good to countless men through my acts of mercy. If, therefore, the true God is the rewarder of all good (of which I have no doubt), I am also certain that He will return the fruits of my benefits, bestowed upon the needy, to my own kin after my death. For thus He says in the 20th chapter of Exodus: *I, He says, am the Lord your God, a jealous God, visiting the sins of the fathers upon the children, even unto the third and fourth generation of those who hate me, and showing mercy unto the thousandth generation of those who love me and keep my commandments.* <note>original: "Ego, inquit, sum Dominus Deus tuus..." - The author quotes the Vulgate Latin Bible to justify his philanthropy as a spiritual investment for his family.</note>

Therefore, in the name of the Lord, by making a beginning in opening my <term>Treasury Chest<note>original: "Arca Thesauraria" - likely referring to his collection of secret recipes or a physical cabinet of wonders.</note></term>, I shall gradually and step-by-step empty it, offering it for common use. From these many treasures, let each person take for their own use what they judge to be profitable: namely, the Physician may take medical things, the Surgeon surgical things, and the Chemist <note>original: "Chymicus" - referring to an alchemist or early pharmacist.</note> chemical things, according as each finds whatever is suitable for his purpose. 

Each person, of whatever rank, will find things with which he can be satisfied; so much so, that whatever is not found in the First, Second, or Third <term>Century<note>original: "Centuria" - a common book format of the era where secrets or recipes were organized into groups of one hundred.</note></term> may be encountered in the remaining ones. For this reason, if ten Centuries are not enough, I shall add another ten or more, so that I may cast off from myself all those cares and anxieties—

-># PREFACE<-

*...dens, with which the guardianship of such great treasures has troubled and moved me for so many years. May Almighty God grant me such talent, health, and strength, and prolong my life until that point, so that I may be able to complete this work for His honor, and for the help, comfort, and benefit of the entire Human Race. Amen.*

<meta>This page begins the first "Century" (a collection of 100 sections) of Johann Rudolf Glauber's General Appendix, serving as a retrospective explanation of his previous chemical and medical works.</meta>

# IN THE NAME OF THE MOST HOLY
# Trinity, I begin to write the First
# Century of my General Appendix,
# or the Explanation of all my
# Writings published until now.

### *I. Concerning Fire, and Salt, and what Alchemy is.*

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring floral and vine motifs within a square border.</image-desc>

ALCHEMY is the science and art of destroying, cleansing, and—through a unique craftsmanship—converting immature and impure Metals into a better form and appearance by means of Fire and Salt, according to the words of Paracelsus <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> who said: "Everything that is something must be converted into nothing, and every nothing must be converted into something." Likewise: "Corruption renders a good thing perfect." This is to be understood equally regarding Particular and Universal Operations <note>In alchemy, "Particular" operations refer to the transformation of specific metals, while "Universal" refers to the creation of the Philosopher's Stone or a panacea for all things.</note>.

However, it is not necessary for me to treat the Particular transmutation of Metals through Fire and Salt at great length here, because that has already been done long ago in the Second Part of *The Miracle of the World* <note>original: "Miraculi Mundi"</note>, and also in the Fifth Part of *The Prosperity of Germany* <note>original: "Prosperitatis Germaniæ"</note>.

<summary>Johann Rudolf Glauber introduces his General Appendix by defining alchemy as the art of refining metals through fire and salt, citing the principles of Paracelsus. He references his previous major works, The Miracle of the World and The Prosperity of Germany, as foundational texts for these processes.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Fire and Salt, Paracelsus, Metal Transmutation, Johann Rudolf Glauber, Chemical Philosophy, Refining Metals</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page begins the second demonstration of the "First Century," continuing the author's exploration of the spiritual and physical properties of elements.</meta>

# First Century.

<note>The term "Century" here refers to a collection of one hundred observations or propositions, a common format for scholarly works in the 17th century.</note>

->In the Sun and in Salt is Everything<-
<note>original: "In Sole et sale Omnia"</note>

### II. A Demonstration, by which it is proved that Fire and Salt are the most noble of God’s Creatures, and that within Fire the purest Salt is hidden, and within Salt the most effective Fire.

I have demonstrated clearly enough in my little work *On the Nature of Salts* <note>original: "de Naturâ Salium"</note> that Fire and Salt are the most noble of God’s creatures. However, it is more than sufficiently known that a subtle <term>Saline Spirit</term><note>In alchemy, a "spirit" refers to a volatile, active essence or vapor that gives a substance its specific properties.</note> is present in every fire, and that a great difference exists between the heat of the Sun and our own culinary <insert>kitchen</insert> fire regarding their respective Saline Spirits. But how to capture such a pure Saline Spirit of the Sun, to concentrate it, and to render it corporeal, palpable, and visible, remains hidden and unknown to us because of our sins; for God reserves such great mysteries for those alone by whom He is honored, and fear—

<summary>This page introduces a philosophical demonstration arguing that fire and salt are divine elements containing hidden spiritual essences, specifically contrasting the "Saline Spirit" of the sun with that of common fire.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, fire, salt, Saline Spirit, solar heat, divine mysteries, Nature of Salts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the elemental and spiritual nature of fire, following the previous page's introduction to its power. Catchword "homi-" suggests the next page begins with "homines" (men).</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

...and is feared. For God himself uses no better likeness than **Fire**, to which the Ancients exhibited divine Honor, and by the aid of which they performed all their sacrifices. Thus among the Chaldeans <note>Ancient people of Mesopotamia known for astronomy and magic</note>, Fire and God are called by one and the same name, *Esh* <note>original: "Efch"; Hebrew word for fire</note>, and among the Pagans the principal Philosophers—indeed, even Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy</note> himself—judged the Sun to be God and worshiped it as God. These things are found expressed in Mutius <note>Likely referring to a classical source on theology</note> regarding the *Nature of the Gods*, and therefore it is not necessary that they be repeated here. It must, however, be well noted that God always appeared to the Saints under the appearance of Fire, and spoke with them out of it. This fact is full of the greatest mysteries, yet observed by few; I shall declare it more plainly and fully in a specific little work concerning the *Concentration of Heaven and Earth*.

I assert, therefore, that it is hardly possible for the admirable—indeed, incredible—Nature of Fire to be described without the revelation of the highest Mysteries of God. Therefore, it is better that such great Secrets be passed over in silence than that they be cast like precious pearls before swine, who are accustomed to receive them with laughter and proclaim them to be nothing but the mere sophistries of triflers. This is clearly evident from I. H. S.’s <note>Likely a contemporary critic or rival alchemist</note> description of the **Philosopher's Stone**, in which he says: "Nature, in the bowels of the Earth, does not use glass, vessels, fire, salt, urine, and the like," and that the Universal <term>Universal Medicine: a substance capable of curing all ills and perfecting metals</term> can be prepared quite conveniently by one who has never handled any Chemical labors, or was never occupied with Fire or Salt. Shame on the man—

### III. Furthermore, it is demonstrated that in all Salts a wonderful fire lies hidden, by whose work many marvelous things are accomplished both in Medicine and Alchemy: And also that it is entirely possible that from Vitriol <note>Iron or copper sulfates</note> the Stone of the Ancient Sages may be prepared; from Saltpeter <note>Potassium nitrate</note> a spiritual Gold and an excellent yellow Tincture; and from common Sea Salt the true Pearl of the Philosophers.

Those who have thoroughly examined and understood the labors of Fire know best that a most powerful Fire lies hidden in all Salts. For by its efficacy and operation, Salts—after the shedding of their earthiness—are reduced back to a fiery force, or a "moist fire," from which they originally arose. Yet one Salt brings forth a fire far different from the fire of another, so much so that this one may be fleeting, while that one is fixed and constant in the fire;

<summary>The author argues that true philosophy requires practical chemical experimentation with fire and salts, claiming that substances like vitriol and saltpeter contain hidden powers capable of producing alchemical wonders.</summary>

Johann Rudolf Glauber <note>Glauber (1604–1670) was a famous German-Dutch alchemist and apothecary, often called the father of chemical engineering.</note>

...others are partly volatile and partly fixed, depending on how the operation has granted them one property or the other. Nevertheless, all such fiery salts <term>Sales ignei: fiery salts, likely referring to concentrated mineral acids</term> can be concentrated by the benefit of art and rendered more effective than they were when produced by a single distillation. For example: if one looks at Vitriol <note>original: "Vitriolum"; a historical term for sulfate minerals, specifically green vitriol or iron(II) sulfate</note> and considers its nature, he will find in actual practice that with the help of a strong fire, that which was there in the beginning can be drawn out of it—namely, a fiery Spirit. This spirit, reduced into a narrow concentration by the aid of external fire, brings forth that internal fire which exerts such great strength that it reduces everything it moistens or surrounds into charcoal. This happens in no other way than if they had been consumed by a common kitchen fire, or by a mirror catching the rays of the Sun and burning everything turned toward it. Regarding these fiery salts, their preparation, and their use, I shall here hand down a few things, beginning with the **Fire of Vitriol** <note>original: "Igne Vitrioli"; here referring to concentrated sulfuric acid</note> and its preparation, the various and manifold uses of which will be explained later in their proper places.

### IV. On the Preparation of the Fire of Vitriol.

Retorts <term>Retorta: a glass or earthenware vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</term> made from the best Earth <note>meaning high-quality refractory clay</note> draw out the Fire of Vitriol through distillation in the following manner. Common Vitriol is calcined <term>calcinatio: heating a substance to high temperatures in air to remove volatile components or oxidize it</term> in earthen pots until it reaches redness; once calcined, it is reduced to a powder, placed into an earthen retort, and set in a furnace, with a massive vessel joined to the neck of the retort...

However, if the retort is larger, all the oil cannot be drawn out in 24 hours, but a longer time is required for this operation, which experience itself will determine. The receiving vessel is then removed, and the Spirits are poured from it into a glass <term>cucurbit</term> <note>A gourd-shaped chemical vessel used for boiling</note>, which is well-protected with lute and placed in a sand bath with an <term>alembic</term> <note>A distilling cap or "helmet" that directs vapors into a cooling tube</note> placed on top. This is done so that the volatile Spirit may be drawn out very slowly and kept in glass vessels for the use to be taught later. Afterward, the <term>Phlegma</term> <note>The watery, tasteless distillate produced during the process</note> is also drawn off and reserved for its own uses, as it possesses its own peculiar powers. Finally, the last Spirit must also be collected in a specific vessel; when fiery drops follow after this spirit ceases, the fire must be gradually removed, and the cucurbit taken out of the cooled sand. In the cucurbit, once the alembic is removed, you will find a fiery oil of a black or reddish color. This must be rectified <note>Purified by redistillation</note> again in a cucurbit using a sand bath, or in a retort well-protected with lute over an open fire, so that it may be rendered more fiery and clear. If anyone, however, should need a greater quantity of the same, he could employ those larger jars prepared from the best earth <note>likely high-quality refractory clay or stoneware</note>, joined together in such a way that the uppermost is placed over the fire, while the others are positioned outside the fire, so that the lowest jar might receive the oil as it exits by descent <term>per descensum: a distillation method where the vapors or liquids are forced downward into a receiver</term>.

### *V. A Test, whether this Oil of Vitriol is properly prepared, sufficiently strong, and suitable for the operation treated here.*

Place a quill or a small piece of wood into the Oil; after you have left it there for a short while, draw it out. If it has been burnt into charcoal, the Oil is properly prepared; if not, it is a sign that some moisture still remains within it, which must again be driven out by fire.

### *VI. Another experiment.*

Insert into the Oil a woolen or linen cloth, or, what is better, a cotton cloth stretched to the width of a finger; then, having removed it and placed it somewhere, drop a few drops of Spirit or Oil of Turpentine <note>original: "Therebinthinæ"</note> upon it...

### VII. Yet Another Proof.

Pour a small amount of Spirit of Wine <note>original: "Spiritus vini"; high-percentage alcohol or ethanol</note> entirely free of phlegm <note>original: "phlegmate"; here meaning watery residue or impurities</note> into a small glass vessel, and gradually add a few drops of this Oil to it: if the Spirit of Wine catches fire and vanishes into smoke, the Oil has been prepared in the proper manner.

NB. I warn anyone to handle this operation cautiously. For in these two Fires—namely the Saline and the Sulphurous—there is a great hidden power, which would seem likely to few if it were revealed to them; nor is it advisable that it should become known to many. These few points teach enough, and more than enough, how such Fires are to be employed in Medicine, Alchemy, and other arts.

But these experiments are sufficient.

### IX. *On the General Use of this Oil in Alchemy.*

By means of this Mineral Fire <note>original: "Igne Minerali"; Glauber's term for the caustic chemical oil he has prepared</note>, transformations of all kinds of things are accomplished. Specifically, it exalts several of the baser metals to a higher grade and renders them more constant <note>meaning more resistant to heat or corrosion</note>, about which more will occur in the following sections.

Universally, several Vegetable, Animal, and Mineral subjects can be reduced into fixed medicines by the operation of this oil, and indeed far more conveniently than by a common fire of wood or coals. Furthermore—and this is a far greater thing—a fiery Tincture <note>a potent alchemical extract or essence</note> is hidden within this very Oil, and is made manifest by the benefit of the art, as Brother Basil <note>referring to Basil Valentine, a legendary alchemical figure</note> and other Philosophers assert.

### X. *On the Use of this Fire in other Arts.*

By the powers of this invisible, and yet essential Fire, most useful things of every kind are brought to fruition; however, it is not within my power to describe them all here at this time, but I am forced to set them aside for another occasion. It seems worthwhile to indicate briefly in this place only that this Fire performs all those things which the fire of coals is otherwise accustomed to effect. It is indeed a Fire, but it does not shine like the fire of wood or coals. He who wishes it to shine, however, must add a subtle sulfur to it, so that he may draw out a visible Fire from it. Protected against the entry of air, this Fire remains hidden for many thousands of years and does not manifest itself unless someone makes it public. It is truly a wonderful Fire, and most fit for accomplishing many incredible things, concerning which we have said enough for this time.

### XI. *Evident Demonstration of such a Fire, latent even in Culinary Salt, which is known to everyone.*

After Plato and several other Philosophers noticed that nothing endowed with life can exist without Salt, and that even corpses are preserved from putrefaction for a long time by its powers, they thought that something Divine was hidden within it.

and they have written. However, they did not teach by what method this Divine and Hidden thing is to be made visible. Without a doubt, those most wise Philosophers <note>Glauber refers here to the ancient alchemists and natural philosophers like Hermes Trismegistus or Paracelsus</note> wished to indicate and denote something of singular excellence by this word.

Because, therefore, God himself is fire <note>original: "Deus ipse ignis est"; a common alchemical trope linking the Creator to the "central fire" of nature</note>, and he never appeared to the Saints in any other form but that of fire, and furthermore, since all Salts are generated in the moist bowels of the Earth by Astral fire, and conversely, from all salts, a true Fire can be extracted by the operation of art <note>the "art" refers to alchemy/chemistry</note> and be made palpable and visible—a fact which undoubtedly did not escape them—it is therefore also likely that it was not without a cause of great importance that they wrote that something Divine or Fiery lies hidden within Salt.

That they did not imply anything better or nobler than that Fiery and Saline Spirit <note>term: Saline Spirit (Spiritus Salis); in modern terms, this often referred to hydrochloric acid, but here it carries a more mystical, energetic meaning</note> can be foreseen by easy conjecture. For if something Divine were hidden in Salt, as they write, it would necessarily follow that this divine spark, once freed from all its earthly bonds, would be far superior in beauty, virtues, efficacy, and power to all earthly things, and would exist as the highest and most precious pearl in the World, second only to the Eternal God himself.

But who will teach us the method of separating so precious a pearl from common and culinary Salt? No one except God alone, or some good friend, who [has received] the know- <insert>ledge</insert> received from God...

Its separation can be accomplished by no other means than by the violence of common fire; namely, when the Salt is mixed with some earthy material so that it cannot flow, and is urged in a retort <note>a glass vessel with a long neck used for distillation</note> with a very strong fire, so that the purer part of the Salt—which is nothing other than a sharp Spirit—may pass out into an attached receiving vessel. In this acidic and pleasant Spirit, a most efficacious Fire lies hidden, to be extracted and concentrated in the following manner.

### XII. On the Preparation of the Fire of Salt.

<margin>Take:</margin> Rectify this acid Spirit of Salt by distilling it through a sand bath, using either a glass gourd-shaped vessel <note>cucurbit</note> or even a Retort. The watery part <note>original: "Phlegma"; the initial weak distillate</note> comes over first, which was poured into the receiving vessel during the first distillation so that the Spirit could be collected more easily. After all the watery part has been drawn out and the acid drops begin to follow, remove that first vessel and catch the Spirit in another container; continue your distillation until all of it has come forth. Endowed with an acid sweetness, it is the performer of many operations which bring much utility in Medicine and Alchemy, as is clear from my various writings, and especially from the *Second Part of Philosophical Furnaces* and the *Consolation for Mariners*. In this sweet and wine-like acid Spirit is hidden an infernal Fire, which—just like a fire of wood or coals—burns everything placed into it, such as Vegetables and Animals, into coals. Furthermore, it burns into white ashes by calcining <note>the process of reducing a metal to a powder through heat or acid</note> all those things which common fire reduces to ashes, such as the immature Metals, Tin, Lead, and the like, when they are placed within it.

<summary>Glauber describes the rectification of the Spirit of Salt through distillation and explains its "infernal" power to chemically burn organic matter and metals into ashes.</summary>

### XIII. *The Concentration of Rectified Spirit of Salt into a Moist and Cold Fire.*

Every **Spirit of Salt** <note>original: "Spiritus Salis"; historically refers to hydrochloric acid</note> consists of two things: namely, **Fire** and **Water**. The Fire binds this Water to itself so closely that it cannot be entirely separated by any distillation or rectification, but always clings to the Fire, no matter how many times it is rectified by distilling. Therefore, if anyone desires to separate the two by rectifying, he must place upon the Spirit of Salt immature **Metallic subjects**. The more immature these are, the purer the Spirit of Salt they render. Such substances are **Calamine Stone** <note>a zinc ore</note>, **Zinc**, and **Iron**. Because of their moist and attracting nature, these substances draw that invisible Fire out of the Spirit of Salt, acting as that Agent of which they are necessarily deprived for their maturation, and without which fiery Agent the Metallic genus can reach no perfection in the Earth.

Therefore, the Spirit of Salt attacks such Metallic subjects placed within it and dissolves them as much as it is able. When this solution is distilled from a glass **retort** <note>a long-necked glass vessel used for distilling</note> through sand with a milder fire, it emits nothing but a mere and tasteless **phlegm** <note>original: "phlegma"; the watery, inactive part of a substance</note>, while the fiery essence itself remains in the retort with that mineral. If this is then urged more and more with an increased fire until it is plainly glowing hot, then that mineral can no longer retain the Fire of the Salt

It posits that these metals act as magnets for the acid's internal heat, which they require for their own "maturation" into perfect metals.</summary>

This Fire is an operator of great effects in Alchemy and Medicine, about which very few know how to discourse. Moreover, it possesses far different properties and qualities than that which is extracted from Vitriol <note>Iron or copper sulfates</note>, of which more will be said later.

And although by the aid of this Fire, incredible things can be performed by Chemists as well as Physicians and other Artisans, it is nonetheless a consuming, destroying, and even a ripening Fire; nor does it possess within itself the least bit of that most noble Union <note>original: "Unionis"; likely referring to the 'Unio Centralis' or the mystical marriage of substances</note> which we mentioned above, and which in this preparation has been converted into such a Fire. If that Union were to be elicited from Salt, in my simple opinion, it should be extracted not by the benefit of Fire, but by the work of Metallic and attracting Subjects.

But although I do not profess to be so skilled a Master, and do not claim for myself the knowledge of extracting so precious a union, I cannot, however, refrain from bringing forth that small spark of Nature which God has granted me, so that everyone may recognize and perceive what admirable Mysteries Salt hides within its vile body.

# First Century.

### XIV. *The method by which that most precious Union of Salt can be made visible, at least in some manner.*

Just as I have advised in my previous writings, that the powers, colors, and virtues of all vegetables, animals, and minerals are found concentrated in Fire and Salt; so I now also affirm and assert the same: that from Salt, by the benefit of Fire, all vegetables, animals, and metals can be increased and propagated infinitely in their own species, nature, and properties, provided that we possess their seeds. For example: I prepare common culinary salt <note>original: "Salem culinarium"</note> through fire in such a way that, having lost its sharpness, it takes on the nature of an Alkali Salt <term>Alkali Salt: a basic, non-acidic salt often derived from plant ashes in early chemistry</term>. I mix some part of it with sterile earth, or even with bare sand, which I moisten with water: into these I sow the seeds of vegetables, so that they may be nourished by that salt and grow. In growing this way, they acquire their own proper shapes, powers, and colors; they appear green, yellow, red, blue, purple, and white, etc., and they have sweet, sour, sharp, or bitter tastes, according as God has bestowed upon each species its own proper nature. This operation proceeds from this single Salt and the fiery Solar rays, tempered by the air.

Therefore, when beasts feed upon these herbs, which grow from this Salt and take their nourishment from it, they are also necessarily nourished and increased by the same,

<margin>XV. Philosophy</margin>

...are nourished, just as these same herbs, growing from the said Salt, provide nourishment and growth to men themselves. If, however, anyone were able to obtain the true seed of Gold, and could increase it with the help of Salt and Fire, he would beyond a doubt attain a great abundance of Gold. But God does not wish for the goat's tail to be extended to the same length as the cow's, lest the goat, puffed up with excessive pride, should strike out its own eyes with too long a tail. <note>A proverbial expression suggesting that divine providence limits human power to prevent self-destruction through vanity.</note> If, therefore, all things—including Gold itself, as well as Silver, Pearls, and precious stones—are hidden in an invisible and secret manner within Salt, and can be made palpable and visible by the assistance of Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to Alchemy or chemistry.</note> and Nature; why should it not also be possible for the most excellent Medicine and the most precious Pearl of the Wise <note>original: "Unio Sapientum"; a term for the Philosopher's Stone or the ultimate alchemical achievement.</note> to be drawn out from that same Salt? Truly, common pearls are born from salty waters; if the First Matter <term>Materia Prima: the primitive, formless substance from which all things are created</term> of pearls were not in those waters, in what way could pearls come forth from them? Therefore, so that it may be clearly evident that pearls can also be drawn from Salt by the operation of art—pearls which far excel in beauty, virtue, efficacy, and excellence those pearls which are extracted by fishing from the deep Sea—I shall prescribe here (as far as I am permitted to demonstrate the possibility of the matter) a certain method. From this, anyone will be able to take a firm and certain foundation for considering the matter more accurately.

<summary>Glauber argues that because Salt contains the "First Matter" of all things, it can be used to artificially create gold, silver, and superior pearls, provided the alchemist follows natural laws.</summary>

# First Century.

### XV. The Operation for Eliciting the Philosophical Union from Salt.

In common water, dissolve as much common salt <note>Sodium chloride</note> as you wish; the greater the quantity you take, the more you will obtain. Likewise, in *Aqua Fortis* <note>Nitric acid; literally "strong water"</note> dissolve one or two *lotones* <note>A historical unit of weight, roughly equivalent to half an ounce or 15-16 grams</note> of Silver. Pour this solution of Moon <note>original: "Lunae"; the alchemical name for silver</note> into the salt dissolved in water, and stir both solutions several times so that it becomes white and like milk. For Silver cannot endure the Salt, but receding from it, seeks the bottom and resides there in the form of a snowy powder, which must be separated by pouring off the water and then dried. This Silver powder has extracted the spiritual and Philosophical Gold, or the so-called "precious pearl," from the salt water. Indeed, Diana <note>The goddess of the moon and hunt, here representing Silver</note> knows how to fish for pearls in the salty sea no less than she knows how to hunt wild beasts in the green forests. This Union, however, is rendered corporeal and visible in the following manner.

### XVI. How the Acquired Union is Rendered Visible.

It must be noted that this Silver powder, by itself and without the admixture of other fixed Salts, returns to its original Silver form through melting only with great difficulty; instead, it flows like Salt and penetrates any vessels, or even vanishes into smoke. For the spirit of the Salt

<meta>This page continues Johann Rudolf Glauber's discussion on the volatile nature of silver when treated with specific salts, following his previous descriptions of chemical preparations.</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

The spirits render the silver <note>original: "Argentum"</note> so fluid and fleeting that it becomes entirely mercurial. Consequently, its more delicate and noble part can be very easily separated from the coarser part through distillation, provided this could be done using glass, earthen, or metallic vessels.

When this "Mercury of Silver" <note>original: "Mercurius Lunæ"; a term used by alchemists to describe silver that has been rendered volatile or liquid like quicksilver</note> is melted in an open crucible, it vanishes into smoke. If placed in a glass retort, it refuses to yield to the fire; if the heat is increased too much, it liquefies the glass and destroys both the vessel and the silver together.

If earthen vessels are used, this same Mercury penetrates them unharmed, just as oil soaks through leather. When it has departed, the salts also vanish into smoke, leaving behind only small grains of silver adhering to the vessel. In this way, a loss occurs which renders the sublimation <term>Sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as a solid</term> useless.

There is also no use for iron vessels here, because the salts mixed with the silver attack the iron. They release the silver, returning it to its original solid body, and emit nothing except a little spirit of salt <note>original: "Spiritus Salis"; known today as hydrochloric acid</note>. Thus, no separation occurs, but the pure and impure parts remain mixed together.

In order to avoid these inconveniences, I attempted many methods in vain. At last, I observed that if a certain material is added to this most penetrating Mercury of Silver—one which hinders its dissolving power and its melting in such a way that it is changed into a porous mass—then, by the benefit of fire, its separation would be easy.

<summary>Glauber describes the technical difficulties of distilling "Mercury of Silver," noting how it escapes or destroys glass, earth, and iron containers. He concludes that a stabilizing material must be added to create a porous mass for successful separation.</summary>

In the Name of the Lord, therefore, add to your fishing net—that is, to the Mercury of the Moon <note>original: "Mercurio Lunæ"; likely a silver amalgam or a specific mercurial preparation associated with the Moon/Silver</note>—a material of the proper weight and measure which admits no melting and does not allow the Mercury of the Moon to flow together. Such materials are wooden coals <note>charcoal</note> reduced to a fine powder. When these are mixed with the Mercury of the Moon, you shall fill your distilling vessel—whether it be made of earth, iron, or glass (which is the best of all)—up to the halfway point. When they have ceased, remove the cooled vessel, and in it you will find the remains of the Mercury which did not ascend, reduced into bodily Silver, or at least such remains that, with the addition of Borax in a crucible, are easily reduced into Silver and contain a certain amount of Gold. However, guard that subtle and pure matter which ascended during the distillation as a precious treasure, and meditate upon the way in which you might fix this precious Pearl <note>original: "Unionem"; a term often used for a large pearl or a unique chemical union</note> according to the Art, and convert it into a fusible and penetrating stone <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note>.

Therefore, I wished only to indicate here those things which I have seen with my own eyes and touched with my own hands. Another may take the risk at his own discretion and see what [results...]

### XVII. *A Easier Method for Achieving the Philosophical Union.*

If you are desirous of acquiring the Philosophical Union <note>The "Philosophical Union" refers to the alchemical marriage or the successful combination of substances into a unified whole, often leading to the creation of the Stone.</note> in a more facile manner, you must work in this way.

To a fifth part, or a half-ounce <term>lotoni: a "loth," a Germanic unit of weight equal to about 14-15 grams</term> of the Mercury of Silver <note>original: "Mercurii Lunae"; refers to a specific preparation of mercury derived from or reacted with silver</note>, add a little charcoal powder. Place the joined materials into a small glass vessel, which is to be set in a crucible and surrounded by sand to the height defined by the material within the glass itself. Upon the mouth of the glass, place a small piece of some other glass so that it is well covered. Then, place a smaller, inverted crucible over that small glass in such a way that its outer and uppermost edges, submerged in the said sand, keep all air away from that small glass. Having fitted the crucibles together in this manner and containing the enclosed small glass, place them among wooden coals and heat them until they remain glowing for a quarter of an hour. Once cooled, take them out; you will find a small amount raised by sublimation <note>The process where a solid turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</note>. The remainder, melted by means of Borax <note>A flux used to facilitate the melting of metals</note>, will yield Silver pregnant with Gold—yet without profit, as this only demonstrates what can be done in the interval of half an hour. However, what profit may be acquired from this very operation shall be indicated presently.

Furthermore, here it is perceived how a most beautiful <term>Union<note>original: "Unio"; likely referring to a "pearl" or a unique alchemical fusion</note></term> comes forth, although very little of it is produced, because in this labor a not insignificant part of it flies off into the air, and it displays only its colors upon the glass—colors far more beautiful than gold, silver, and precious stones, if one has worked correctly. Nor will there be any painter who could express them by imitation or painting.

For this turn, take what I say in good part, and by meditating, weigh such a great matter more accurately. Pray, work, and seek; by seeking such things, you will find what you would never have believed before.

The brothers of ignorance, my enemies, will object to me here and say that these most elegant colors did not draw their origin from the <term>Salt<note>original: "Sal"; in alchemy, Salt represents the principle of fixity and the body of a substance</note></term>, but from the <term>Silver<note>original: "Argentum"</note></term>. To these I respond briefly: that they were indeed extracted from the Salt with the help of Silver, but they do not belong to the Silver by themselves. For if they were of Silver, they would also be dissolved by <term>Aqua Fortis<note>literally "strong water"; nitric acid, used to dissolve metals</note></term>; since this does not happen, they are not Silver, but the pure soul of the Salt. So that this may be established by a more evident proof, I bring forward the solution of <term>Saturn<note>original: "Saturnus"; the alchemical name for Lead</note></term>, which itself, without Silver, can fish out the same Union from the Salt, if one has worked in the same manner I just indicated. I also add this: that I shall later teach the method by which Saturn can fish out pure, bright <term>Gold<note>original: "Aurum"</note></term> from all Salts.

Let us return now to the moist and cold Fire.

### XVIII. *On the Preparation of the Moist and Cold Fire of Saltpeter.*

<margin>Take:</margin> Two parts of potter's clay <note>original: "Terræ figulinæ"</note>, unmixed with any sand and well-burnt, and one part of the best purified Saltpeter. When all the spirits have ceased to exit and have been collected by the water, the receiving vessel is removed, and the acidic <term>Spirit of Nitre</term><note>Nitric acid</note> is poured into a strong glass container. Once freed from superfluous <term>phlegm</term><note>The watery, inactive byproduct of distillation</note> by the work of distillation and rectified, it is employed for medical and chemical uses. Mention is made of its operation and powers in the Second Part of my *Furnaces*, and in Schroeder’s *Pharmacopoeia*. Furthermore, here is the method for extracting and concentrating the Fire from this Spirit.

Pour this Spirit of Nitre onto the powder of <term>Calamine stone</term><note>Zinc ore, historically used in making brass</note>, or onto Zinc reduced into small granules, so that it may dissolve as much as it can. This fiery smoke of <term>Saltpeter</term><note>Potassium nitrate</note>, as well as of <term>Vitriol</term><note>Sulfuric acid or metal sulfates</note> and common Salt, burns all herbs, grass, leaves, and flowers, and whatever it touches, just as if they had been consumed by the strong heat of the sun or fire.

And this is the nature of the moist and cold Fire of Saltpeter.

### XIX. *Concerning the Moist Fire of Alum.*

Alum <note>Latin: *Alumen*; a naturally occurring sulfate mineral used as an astringent and in dyeing</note>, through the work of distillation and concentration, also brings forth an effective Fire. In its effectiveness and power, it is very similar to that which Vitriol <note>Iron or copper sulfates, often called "green" or "blue" vitriol</note> produces; however, it does not match it in quantity because of the excessive Earth <note>Refers to the solid, inactive mineral residue</note> in which it abounds. Nevertheless, if some of the other Salts are added to it, it yields its Fire correctly and in due order.

### XX. *Concerning the Moist and Cold Fire of Sulfur.*

Although Sulfur does not find a place in the order of Salts—because it rejects solution in water—it nevertheless contains a hidden Vitriolic Salt. This salt does not manifest itself until the sulfur's oilier substance has been removed through burning. By this operation, the Salt is thinned and, like a sharp smoke, is carried upward with the flame, so much so that this sharp sulfurous Spirit <note>Sulfuric or sulfurous acid vapors</note> burns everything it touches, after the manner of all those Fires which are made from Salts. For the sake of acquiring this Vitriolic and sulfurous Spirit, the flame of the Sulfur must be caught in a certain Alembic <note>A chemical apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for distilling</note> made of glass or earth specifically for this operation, in which that Vitriolic Spirit of Salt condenses itself and flows out.

In the case of Silver, a smaller abundance of Sulfur or Vitriol is found than in Copper. Gold has little Vitriol or Sulfur—indeed, plainly none at all—if it has reached its highest maturity, because it is then found pure and malleable, and requires no further smelting or liquefaction. By what method such a Sulfur <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and "soul" principle of a substance</note>, similar in all respects to the Mineral kind, may be extracted from any herb or any wood, I have already handed down in my little work, *On the Nature of Salts*, and in the *Second Part of the Miracle of the World* <note>original: "Miraculi Mundi"</note>, and I shall demonstrate it below with much clearer evidence.

Let us proceed to Animals and Vegetables, and consider whether in those very things that same ripening Fire <note>original: "Ignis"</note> may be encountered, and whether it can also be extracted from them and rendered visible.

Fill some glass, stone, or earthen distilling vessel <note>original: "vas distillatorium"</note> with some dry wood,

Concerning these wonderful mutations of Nature and conversions from one species into another, many things are found scattered throughout my writings. In the First Part of the *Continuation of the Miracle of the World* <note>original: "Miraculi Mundi"; likely referring to the author's previous alchemical treatise.</note>, it is openly described in what way an abundance of this kind of "Fire" can be acquired from woods; however, its concentration is handed down here. In general, it should be noted here that one wood or one herb abounds more in such "Fire" than another. The older a piece of wood or an herb is...

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

is, and the longer the solar rays have acted upon it, the more Fire it supplies. This is evident from the Vine, which receives such copious rays of this kind, and therefore surpasses all other vegetables with a greater and stronger Fire. The proof of this is not only its burning Spirit <note>original: "Spiritus ardens," referring here to ethanol or brandy distilled from wine</note>, but also its Tartar, or Tartareous Salt, which is almost entirely Fire, and yet cannot be made manifest without distillation and calcination <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to oxidize it or remove volatile parts</term>. Therefore, so that it may be brought into the open and rendered visible, the following operation must be used.

### XXII. *The method of manifesting the Fire of the Vine.*

Fill a glass retort <term>retort: a spherical glass vessel with a long, downward-sloping neck used for distillation</term> with common Tartar, and draw out its volatile Spirit by distilling, as well as the oil, which you shall separate in the proper manner. In this oil there are great virtues, of which I made mention in the Second Part of *The Furnaces* <note>referring to Glauber's famous work "Furni Novi Philosophici"</note>. The Spirit is to be rectified in a Water-Bath <note>original: "Balneo," referring to a bain-marie</note> so that only the fiery substance comes out, and the useless phlegm <term>phlegm: an alchemical term for the watery, inactive byproduct of distillation</term> is left at the bottom. Upon the fixed Salt which remains in the retort—after it has been heated white-hot with a strong fire and calcined—the rectified Spirit must be poured, and then drawn out again by distillation. In this way, that fixed Salt retains the remaining useless phlegm within itself, and the Spirit acquires greater fiery powers to perform wonderful effects in Medicine, which my writings teach.

### XXIII. *Another method of eliciting a far stronger Fire from Tartar.*

Dissolve that Alkalized Salt <note>Potassium carbonate derived from plant ashes</note>, from which the Spirit was drawn off during purification, in a little water so that it becomes an extremely sharp lye. Pour one pound of this lye into a gourd-shaped vessel <term>cucurbit: a glass vessel used in distillation</term> containing two pounds of white Tartar <note>The crust-like sediment from white wine barrels</note> which has been reduced to a powder. Secure the still-head <term>alembic: the cap of the distilling apparatus</term> to the gourd with a strong sealant <note>original: "luto," a paste of clay and fiber used to prevent leaks</note>, place the joined vessels in a sand bath, and gradually apply fire from below. If you have operated correctly, you will obtain a most subtle Fire, a single droplet of which burns the tongue just as if it had been touched by a glowing white-hot iron.

How many wonderful things can be accomplished with this Fire, I have already indicated in other places in my writings.

---

### XXIV. *A method for eliciting a still more vehement Fire from Tartar.*

Take equal parts of raw Tartar and the Regulus of Mars <term>Regulus of Mars: metallic antimony reduced using iron</term>, which you shall mix by pounding together. Place them in a crucible so fortified with sealant and closed that it admits no air; heat it to a glowing white heat for one hour, and then remove it.

From all these things, it becomes known to anyone that a vehement Fire lies hidden and concealed within Vegetables <note>In early chemistry, "vegetables" referred to all plant-based substances</note>, serving for the performance of many admirable things in Medicine, Alchemy, and other arts, the narration of which, due to the constraints of time and of this treatise—

The brevity of this treatise commands us to refrain from further elaboration at this time.

Furthermore, it must be seen whether animals also possess this same Fire, and by what means one might become a participant in it.

### XXV. *The Preparation and Concentration of Fires from Animals.*

Just as the Vine is the most noble of all vegetables, so too is Man esteemed by all endowed with judgment as the most noble among the animals; the reality of the matter confirms this to be true by its own clear testimony. Therefore, we pass over all other animals in silence and here indicate the following method for preparing the Fire lying hidden in man alone.

The ancient philosophers called the great world the Macrocosm <note>from the Greek 'makros' (large) and 'kosmos' (world)</note>, and man the Lesser World, or Microcosm <note>from the Greek 'mikros' (small)</note>. Having made a comparison, they determined that those things found in the Great World are also found in the Lesser World—that is, in Man. From this, they also unanimously believed and recorded for posterity that the Life of both the Greater and Lesser World consists in a Saline Spirit <note>original: "Salino Spiritu"; likely referring to a volatile salt or life-force</note>, and that this spirit dominates more in one place and less in another. Nor is there anyone who would wish to, or could, deny that the entire earth is filled with Salt as if with its own balsam, and that by it, the minerals in the very bowels of the earth—

<summary>Glauber argues that humans, as the 'Microcosm,' contain a concentrated life-force or 'Saline Spirit' similar to the 'Macrocosm' of the earth, setting the stage for extracting alchemical fire from human sources.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Rudolf Glauber, Microcosm, Macrocosm, Saline Spirit, Alchemical Fire, Animal Kingdom, Salt of the Earth</keywords>

just as vegetables are born upon the surface of the earth. Nevertheless, the Salt of the Great World <note>The "Great World" or Macrocosm refers to the universe or the earth itself.</note> is found nowhere more abundantly than in water, or the seas; this is so well known that it requires no further proof.

The same must also be understood regarding the Small World, namely Man <note>The "Small World" or Microcosm refers to the human being, viewed as a reflection of the entire universe.</note>. Although his entire body, or rather all its parts, abound with Salt—his true balsam—a greater abundance of this salty and balsamic Spirit is found in the flesh than in the bones, and more in the blood than in the flesh. However, the greatest amount is found in the bladder, or the "salty sea" of the Lesser World, a fact which is hidden from no one. It is customary that necessary things should not be sought in remote places, but in those nearby, where they are easiest to find.

Hence, because Salt is encountered nowhere in Man more copiously than in his urine, we rightly seize the opportunity to seek and concentrate that Fire <note>likely referring to Phosphorus, which was famously first isolated from urine by alchemists in the 17th century</note> within it, and we employ the following operation.

### XXVII. Observations concerning the preparation of the Animal Fire.

For the most part, such a Fire is summoned forth from human urine—which has been allowed to putrefy by itself for the space of several weeks—by means of distillation, and is converted through rectification into a moist and fiery essence, just as the Second Part of my *Furnaces* <note>Referring to Glauber's famous work, "Furni Novi Philosophici" (New Philosophical Furnaces)</note> indicates. In that same place, I taught a easier method of eliciting this same Fire from Sal Ammoniac <term>Sal Armoniacus: ammonium chloride, often derived from organic sources like soot or urine</term>, which is prepared from urine and distilled and rectified with the addition of a strong lye.

I have also shown a way of preparing the same Fire from that same Sal Ammoniac by the addition of Calamine stone <term>lapis Calaminaris: zinc ore, used here as a reagent</term> and distillation through a retort. Spirits prepared correctly by these methods are equally useful in Medicine, Alchemy, and other arts; for when they are properly made, they are all good, by whatever method they are prepared.

However, although these fleeting Animal Fires serve for bringing remarkable things to effect, and even the fixed Salt of urine can be concentrated into another kind of fire through distillation and rectification; nevertheless, they are still far distant from that true Philosophical Fire <note>original: "Igne illo Philofophico vero"</note> which the Ancients concealed with such great care and such great anxiety, because in these...

During the preparations, the best part of the Fire flies away and is lost. However, I say this: that these concentrated fiery Spirits of urine <note>original: "Spiritus urinæ"; likely ammonium carbonate or ammonia solution derived from fermented urine</note>, prepared as I have taught, are indeed able to accomplish all those things which I have attributed to them, and shall yet attribute; but one thing, and that the best, they do not bring to effect: namely, that they might coagulate the concentrated Fire of the Vine <note>original: "concentratum Vitis Ignem"; referring to highly distilled alcohol or Spirit of Wine</note>. This coagulation stands as no small key to the composition of the Universal Medicine. 

For when the Spirit of urine acquires this nature—so that by coagulating the most subtle Spirit of Wine, it changes it into a Salt when poured upon it—then, by this same Salt rightly prepared, the soul is extracted from Gold. This Salt also, if it is changed and converted into a dry and sweet Salt and fixed, possesses the powers of a most noble and great Medicine for use in healing. 

Anyone who is enlightened by even the smallest natural light will be able to perceive with little trouble what lies hidden beneath this Salt. From the Vine comes the purest substance, the Spirit of Wine, which beyond all other things strengthens the heart of man, as well as the brain and his other members. The Spirit of urine is the purest and most subtle Mercurial and Animal salt, having no equal in penetrating, opening, and dissolving. Therefore, one can easily conjecture what this subtle, Mercurial, Animal, and penetrating Fire, joined to the purest Vegetable Sulphur <note>original: "Sulphuri Vegetabili"; here meaning the flammable principle of alcohol</note>—that is, the Spirit of Wine—might effect in Medicine, when it is changed together with it into a dry remedy.

<meta>This page continues Johann Rudolf Glauber's chemical instructions, specifically focusing on the volatile nature of "Animal Fire" (ammonia) and its role in solidifying spirits.</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

In order to point out the source of error and demonstrate the reason why a Spirit of Wine <note>original: "Spiritus Vini"; ethanol or high-proof alcohol</note> that can coagulate into a Salt is so rarely prepared, I advise everyone to take the following reasoning into account.

First, it must be noted that only the most subtle part of the Spirit of Urine <note>original: "Spiritus urinæ"; an aqueous solution of ammonia</note>, and not the thicker part, is suitable for the coagulation of the Spirit of Wine. If, therefore, during its preparation—whether through the operator's negligence or lack of skill—the most subtle part is lost, it is by no means possible for the remaining thicker and more dreg-filled portion to facilitate that coagulation. This most subtle Spirit does not only vanish during distillation through glassware that is insufficiently sealed, but a great abundance of it is also lost before distillation. This occurs when the urine, collected over time, is forced to stand and wait too long, so that the Spirit gradually exhales and vanishes into the air; this happens especially when it is gathered in the heat of Summer or the cold of Winter. For that "Fire" <note>Glauber refers to the volatile ammonia as a "Fire" due to its pungent, active nature.</note> is impatient of any extremes; it is driven away by slight heat or cold. Consequently, it requires no more convenient time for collection than March and May, or September and October, in which months the air is temperate, being neither too hot nor too cold. These four months, therefore, are the most convenient for collecting urine and extracting the <term>Animal Fire</term> <note>original: "Ignis Animalis"; the volatile principle or "spirit" derived from animal sources, here specifically ammonia.</note> from it. Furthermore, <term>quicklime</term> <note>original: "calx viva"; calcium oxide, used here to help release the ammonia gas from the liquid.</note> must be added to the putrefied urine that is to be distilled, so that the tasteless water may be more easily separated from that fleeting Fire, which does not happen if it is distilled by itself.

<summary>Glauber explains that the failure to create "Stone of Wine" often results from losing the volatile "Animal Fire" (ammonia) during collection or distillation, advising that urine be gathered in temperate months and treated with quicklime.</summary>

I did not wish to pass over these few points in silence here, for the sake of the Reader and those studious of good Medicines.

However, how Metals may be improved by this <term>Animal Fire<note>Latin: Ignis Animalis. We conclude here the preparation and concentration of the Animal and Vegetable Fires, whose admirable virtues and powers in Medicine and Alchemy, as well as other useful Arts, shall be revealed here in order, as far as the account of time will allow. Truly I say, that by the manifestation of these Fires I have lit a great torch for a perverse World: yet I have no doubt that the greatest part of mortals will not give credit to this light, but will turn their backs to it, judging such a light—by which they might allow themselves to be illuminated—unworthy of their sight.

For that reason, God does not grant the manifestation of such great things to the proud, lest they exalt themselves with greater pride, if they should attain a greater and clearer light than that which is known, common, and shines for every peasant. The greatest part of men, and especially those who puff themselves up with the title of wisdom and erudition, are entirely ignorant of what Light and Darkness are. Let the lives of the ancient Saints be diligently inspected; let the actions of those who were illustrated by Divine light be examined; they will certainly be found to have performed great and supernatural miracles, which were impossible to be done by the whole perverse world, filled as it is with darkness.

I ask you to look upon the elementary Sun, as well as the fire of wood, and consider the powers, light, and services of both, which all creatures—and especially the Human Race itself—are compelled to use for their own safety. Could even the smallest blade of grass come forth, or any little worm be born and live, without the Sun? Could any craft <note>original: "opificium," referring to any manual art, trade, or manufacturing process</note> be practiced without the aid of common fire? If it did not exist, we would be forced to feed upon uncooked herbs and raw meat, like wild beasts. Indeed, all social interaction and business among men would be abolished if we were deprived of earthly fire and light.

If in some entire city, or province, or even a whole kingdom, there were one single man who alone could make others partakers of fire and light, would there not be the greatest gathering of all people to him? But because fire is known to everyone, and each person knows how to strike it from flints <note>original: "ex silicibus," referring to the common method of starting fires with flint and steel</note> with easy effort, it is held in no value; just as all those things which become common are usually held in no value, no matter how precious they might actually be. The same has happened to fire, which, although it ought to be highly esteemed, is nevertheless considered as nothing because it is common and ordinary.

However, just as the common fire known to everyone provides much good to mortals through many very useful operations—for they can by no means do without its use—so also I assert that those ingenious and hidden fires <note>original: "artificiosos & occultos illos Ignes," likely referring to chemical reagents, acids, or specific furnace temperatures used in alchemy</note> ought to be highly valued, inasmuch as by them, for the preparation of effective...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the essential nature of chemical fires, specifically those derived from salts, which were introduced in the preceding section of the First Century.</meta>

# First Century

The Physician can hardly do without effective medicines, and the Chemist can never do without the transmutation <note>The transformation of one substance into another, specifically base metals into gold or silver</note> of lesser metals into better ones; without their help, neither will achieve anything of great importance in chemical labors.

He who suffers from an ignorance of such Fires, what shall he accomplish in metallic works? Dwelling in cold and darkness, he is afflicted by the same difficulty as a baker or a beer-brewer who, in the winter time, is destitute of wood or unable to use water frozen into ice; the former cannot knead bread, though he has the finest flour, and the latter cannot brew beer, even if he abounds in the best grain.

So it is also with Alchemical Fires, the lack of which ensures that we do not practice the most noble Alchemy with any profit, but rather perceive damage from it. As daily experience bears witness, a hundred men are usually ruined before it happens that a single one acquires any riches for himself from Alchemy. The blame for this misfortune is not to be transferred to the impossibility of the Art, but rather imputed to a lack of those moist, cold, and maturing Fires <note>A reference to "potential" or chemical heat found in salts, which can dissolve or "cook" substances without a literal flame</note> extracted from Salts. How these ought to be employed for the improvement of metals, as well as for Medicine and other Arts, will be taught partly in this and partly in the following Centuries.

<term>Transmutation of Metals</term>: The process of changing base metals like lead or iron into noble metals like gold.

<term>Ignorance of Fires</term>: Lack of knowledge regarding the various types of heat (dry, moist, potential) used in laboratory work.
<term>Alchemy</term>: The art of perfecting substances.
<term>Salts</term>: Chemical compounds used as reagents to dissolve or alter minerals.
<term>Improvement</term>: The purification or "ennobling" of substances.

I therefore boldly repeat my many-times-reiterated affirmations, and I firmly assert—insofar as the labor of great expense has made known to me thus far—that in Alchemy <note>original: "Halchymiâ"</note>, nothing of utility or advantage is to be expected without these moist and cold Fires of Salts. To put it briefly, if the imperfect Metals of common and coarse Salts, and

By the same reasoning, in the preparation of Medicines <note>original: "Medicamentorum"</note> and other arts, the use of Salts will not be of such great efficacy as the employment of common Spirits. However, because these common spirits are for the most part still imbued with much phlegm <note>term: phlegm; in early chemistry, this refers to the watery, inactive part of a distilled liquid</note>, they necessarily do not exert such great power as those concentrated and fiery Spirits which have been freed from all phlegm.

An example is found in the Solar rays <note>original: "Solares radii"</note>, which do not emit as much heat when they are mixed with humid air; just as green wood, soaked with moisture, does not burn with as much fervor as dry and seasoned wood is accustomed to do. Indeed, if the hot rays of the Sun are concentrated by some hollow mirror <note>original: "speculo"</note> that increases the fire, or if the fires of coals are concentrated by the strong blast of bellows and, as it were, forced into a narrow space, they achieve ten times—nay, a hundred times—more than those which are not concentrated in such a manner. Truly, the more more intensely and tightly those powers of the Solar rays or other fires are concentrated, the greater, stronger, and sharper the heat they bring forth.
