If the mirror has a length of one foot, it will only ignite wood. If, however, its diameter extends to a length of two feet, it will melt tin, lead, and other metallic materials of that kind which are easy to liquefy, such as <term>Bismuth</term><note>original: Bismuthum</note>, <term>Zinc</term><note>original: Zincum</note>, <term>Cobalt</term><note>original: Koboltum</note>, etc. If the size of the diameter in the same mirror contains four feet, the solar rays—taking on stronger increments—will melt silver and copper, and will render iron itself so glowing hot that it can be worked with a hammer just as if it had been heated by burning coals. This effect of the solar rays is to be ascribed to the instrument of concentration, just as the heat of coals is ascribed to the compression of the bellows or the wind.

The same must be understood regarding our concentrated and "moist" fires <note>Glauber refers here to concentrated acids or chemical solvents as "liquid fires"</note>, which ought to be compared not only with the common rays of the sun or the heat of a kitchen fire, but even with those solar rays concentrated by a mirror and the fiery heat of coals forced by the blasts of bellows. From this, it must necessarily be that they possess greater powers than common salts and their watery spirits; anyone who possesses a sharper intellect can sufficiently grasp and believe this. Simple peasants and craftsmen see this with their eyes and touch it with their hands, knowing well that the subtle, hot, pleasant spirits of <term>Wine</term><note>original: Vini; ethanol</note> and <term>Beer</term><note>original: Cerevisiæ</note>—which provide strength to the heart—perform ten times more when they are freed from all moisture through distillation and concentrated through rectification, than if they were still with

<summary>Glauber uses the analogy of a concave burning mirror to explain how concentrating chemical "spirits" and salts increases their potency, just as focusing sunlight can melt metals.</summary>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the chemical transformation of substances, likely following a section on the dilution or preparation of medicinal bases.</meta>

# First Century

If they were mixed with the moisture of wine or beer <note>original: "cerevisiæ"</note>, they would possess no efficacy.

To understand the matter more clearly, consider grapes, grain, or the fruits of trees: We eat grapes and tree fruits, such as apples and pears, in the same substance as they are supplied to us by the trees. They do provide us with nourishment, but not of the same kind as that provided by their juice when it has been pressed out, separated from its dregs, and reduced through fermentation into a clear and pleasant drink. In a case of urgent necessity, grain could be used for the sake of nourishment, yet not in the same way as when it is separated from its husks, changed into flour, reduced into a mass with water, sprinkled with salt, fermented, and baked by fire into bread of the finest flavor. By the very same reasoning, grain boiled in plain water provides a drink somewhat better than the water itself; but if it is treated skillfully and brewed into beer <term>cerevisia: an early Latin term for ale or beer</term>, the husks are separated from the purer juice, which afterwards removes even more dregs from itself through fermentation and passes into a nobler nature, providing a sweeter and better drink. If, however, that same juice is later brought to a greater purity through further distillation, and concentrated through rectification <term>rectificatio: the process of refining a substance by repeated distillation to increase its potency</term>, so that the powers of the grain are, as it were, compressed into a narrow space, it will exert far greater powers—being essentially pure Fire <note>original: "merus Ignis"</note>—than the coarse grain, which lacks the ability to exert such great forces.

<summary>The author uses the metaphors of baking bread and brewing beer to explain how chemical processes like fermentation and distillation refine raw matter into a "nobler" and more potent state. He argues that concentrated spirits represent the "pure fire" or essential power of the original plant material.</summary>

<keywords>distillation, fermentation, alchemy, rectification, medicinal potency, beer, wine, bread-making, elemental fire</keywords>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

So it is also arranged with the **concentration of salts** <note>original: "Salium concentratione"</note>, from which their own dregs are snatched away by the help of Art, and the purer parts are converted into a fiery substance and concentrated, performing effects of great importance in Alchemy.

However, that salts commonly destroy metals, as much through the wet way as through the dry way <note>The "wet way" refers to liquid acid dissolution, while the "dry way" refers to high-heat crucible reactions</note>, is known even to the "bleary-eyed and the barbers" <note>A Latin idiom meaning "everyone knows this," or it is common knowledge even to the uneducated</note>. But by what method the destroyed metals can be reduced into nobler bodies than they were before, there has been no one until now who, being skilled in such a craft, has not hidden it with the greatest care. From this it has happened that no benefit has been perceived from metallic transmutation, and Alchemy itself has been held in derision by the most ignorant crowd of the unskillful, just as if it were entirely false and furthest removed from all truth. Thus the learned and unlearned alike, the highest and the lowest, the young and the old, have fallen into the opinion that Alchemy is nothing but a mere dream, an empty fiction, and a certain vain phantom, which has devised the transmutation of imperfect metals into more perfect ones by a most vain fabrication.

Since, however, concerning the truth of this Art, not only so many Pagan Philosophers—in whose number there were even Kings—but also very many among Christians, famous men of both higher and lower status, such as Bishops, Cardinals, and other most learned men, have writ- <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "scripserunt" (have written)</note>

# First Century

I shall do this so that everyone, even those imbued with only a slight knowledge of fire, will be able to obtain some benefit from them with easy effort. I will deal first with common and crude Salts, then with their simple Spirits, and finally with their concentrated Spirits and Fires, which we have taught how to extract from them.

But before I approach the description and defense of this <term>Royal Art</term> <note>original: "Regiam Artem" - a traditional name for Alchemy</note>, I thought I should first indicate the reason why, in the description of the same, several places occur in which words have been omitted, and signs put in their place. This was done, of course, so that the art might be hidden from the unworthy—who must be kept away from it by every means—and made known only to the <term>adepts</term> <note>Those who have already mastered the secrets of alchemy</note> and the <term>sons of Art</term> <note>A traditional term for sincere students or initiates of alchemy</note>. God has not placed all men on the same level, for He wills that one should serve another, and one should rule over another, according as he has been judged worthy. ### XXIX. *An Infallible Practice for changing imperfect Metals into more perfect ones, with the assistance of raw Salts.*

Just as I have already demonstrated clearly a little while ago, and also in other places in my writings, that Salts or the Spirits of Salts are the universal Agent both within the earth and outside of it, promoting the maturation of Metals; so I here again firmly assert and say, that the coarse bodies of Metals are destroyed by Salts and transmuted into nobler Metals. This occurs indeed in various and manifold ways, and more easily or with more difficulty, depending on whether one is more or less experienced in <term>Chemical</term> <note>The original Latin "Chymicis" refers to the early modern art of alchemy and chemistry, which were not yet distinct disciplines.</note> labors. All things here, God willing, I shall manifest—not to just anyone, however, but only to the worthy. I shall act in the manner of clockmakers, who, taking a clock apart, set all its pieces down somewhere without any particular order; a person unskilled in the art will never put them back together or restore them to order, but another, who has previously practiced that craftsman's art, will rejoin all those parts with easy effort and restore them to the original body of the clock.

Therefore, all those who have previously experienced labors to be perfected by Fire, will from these my descriptions...

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

They will easily explain themselves to those who understand, but will be difficult to comprehend for the ignorant crowd, which has performed no **experiments with fire** <note>Latin: *Ignis experimenta*; Glauber refers to laboratory chemistry/alchemy which relies on the furnace</note>, and will look upon what I have written in vain; just as if someone entirely unskilled in reading and writing should look at written letters and not know what they signify or what subject they contain. If such a man wished to be angry with the writer, would he not be doing him an injustice? For the writer is absent from the fault of that man's ignorance, which prevents him from reading those letters he never learned to read.

The same must be understood regarding my writings, which were published not for the sake of just anyone, but only for those who have first learned how to understand such writings.

But to approach the matter itself, and to teach the **improvement of metals** <note>Latin: *Metallorum emendationem*; the process of "curing" base metals to turn them into gold or silver</note> into better forms, I point out the very foundation of the whole business: I say that their true transformation is attempted and expected in vain, unless they are first destroyed and utterly killed. A grain of wheat, as Christ himself said, will never increase or multiply itself unless it first rots in the earth. If, therefore, metals must be destroyed through **putrefaction** <note>The alchemical stage of breaking down a substance until it turns black and "dies"</note>, it is necessary that this be done with the **help of salts**, according to the truth of the Philosopher's axiom: *The corruption of one is the generation of another* <note>A fundamental Aristotelian principle: for something new to be born, something else must be destroyed</note>; the death of one is the life of another. Since, therefore, the metals must die, it is necessary that death be brought upon them by enemies or by things contrary to them, because nothing in natural things—

# First Century

than by artificial means, unless it is killed by its own enemy.

Since Metals <note>original: "Metalla"</note> must therefore be destroyed and killed by Salts <note>original: "Salibus"</note>, their enemies, it is necessary that they be invaded, twisted, and tortured by them for so long, until the Agent <note>the active substance, in this case the salt</note>, as the stronger part, is overcome by the Patient <note>the passive substance being acted upon, here the metal</note>, as the weaker part, and is killed and translated into a better nature; in which Action the patient must not withdraw from the Agent, but must be tortured by unceasing torment.

Whoever, having his enemy in sight, knows himself to be endowed with weaker strength, strives as much as he can to avoid the hostile onset by retreating. However, when every opportunity for fleeing and escaping is snatched away, he is forced to surrender his life to the enemy, who treats the Patient part according to his pleasure, and makes from it whatever he wishes.

Thus is the refinement <note>original: "emendatio"</note> of Metals: although they might be melted together with the Salts, their enemies, they would nevertheless think little of them, and in the very act of fusion <note>melting into a liquid state</note> they would segregate themselves from them, so that each part would preserve its own nature and essence unharmed. If, however, the Salts take away from the Metals the opportunity of fleeing, and shut them up in their prisons <note>likely referring to a sealed crucible or vessel</note> so that they have no way of escaping, but must remain, suffer, and die, then they are possessed by the victory of the Salt, and from the killed Metals, purer and better things are fabricated. ### XXX. *How Metals are slain by their enemies, and transmuted into better things.*

Metals are opposed not by one, but by many enemies; some of these enemies are hostile to certain metals while friendly to others, and vice versa. For example:

Nothing pursues Gold with more hostile hatred than burning Sulfur and sulfurous Salts, such as Alkalis <note>original: "Alcalia"; alkaline salts like potash</note> and crude Tartar <note>original: "Tartarus crudus"; potassium bitartrate deposits from wine barrels</note>. The cause of this hatred is that Gold is nothing but fixed Sulfur <note>In alchemical theory, gold was considered "perfected" or "fixed" sulfur that no longer burned</note>, and therefore it is at mortal odds with all burning Sulfur. Silver and Lead love all Sulfur and all sulfurous Salts, such as Vitriol <note>sulfate minerals</note>, Saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note>, Sal Ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note>, and the like, which they require for their color. On the other hand, they hate common culinary Salt <note>sodium chloride</note>, as they are of a Mercurial nature <note>referring to the "Mercury-Sulfur" theory of metal composition</note> and therefore do not require its help, but desire only Sulfur and tincture. Copper, Iron, and Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum Vivum"; Mercury</note> partake of both natures, namely the Mercurial,

The greater and more violent the enemies are by which the Metals are killed, the more the metals themselves suffer, and the more brilliant and better the bodies are with which they rise again. The whole Art <term>Ars: The Alchemical Art or Great Work</term> therefore consists in this: that the Metals be overwhelmed by their greatest enemies, killed, separated from their enemies after death, and restored to a better life by their best Friends. You have the whole Art; nothing else remains but for you to undertake the work and set your hand to it.

Silver is produced by one and the same process. ### XXXI. *A brief and concise method for extracting fleeting Gold from colored flints, red Talc, Garnets, Sand, Clay, and similar Metallic earths, and rendering it into physical form.*

In the beginning, these Ores or Metallic earths must be heated to a glow, extinguished in cold water, and ground into flour or powder in a mill. Afterward, you shall place the ground materials into a Waldenburg or Cologne pitcher <note>These were types of high-quality stoneware vessels famous for their durability in chemical processes</note>, and pour over them just enough Aqua Regia <term>Aqua Regia: "Royal Water," a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold</term> so that they are merely moistened, and they should be placed with their pitcher over a charcoal fire and heated by being surrounded by it. After the Ores and the Aqua Regia have thoroughly warmed together, as much hot water must be poured over the Ores as will be necessary to extract the Aqua Regia. Place the Ores thus moistened with water into large pots made of the best earth, having several small holes in the bottom, over which blotting paper has been placed to support the Ores so they do not fall through the holes, but allow only the water to pass through. ### XXXII. *How a true Saltpeter can still be obtained with profit from the extracted Ores.*

The ores, having been freed by extraction in the said manner, are to be mixed with an equal weight of <term>Quicklime</term> <note>Latin: calci vivæ; calcium oxide, a caustic alkaline substance.</note> and wood ashes, and are to be thrown into a single heap under some shed, so that it can be moistened from time to time with urine, or if this is lacking, with rain water, whenever they have become dried out. The cause of this manufactured saltpeter is as follows: because <term>Aqua Fortis</term><note>original: "Aqua fortis"; nitric acid</note>, or Aqua Regia, or <term>Spirit of Niter</term><note>original: "Spiritus Nitri"; another term for nitric acid</note> contains within those same waters a sort of "seed" of saltpeter, it possesses a nature such that, like a plant, it takes increase from other salts and multiplies. From this, perhaps, arose the old proverb, **"To sow salt"**, which the ignorant received with mockery, asking how salt can be sown and multiplied when it is dissolved and carried away by rainwater? But it was hidden from them what kind of salt it is, and how it must be sown, which we have demonstrated here, while simultaneously asserting the truth of the old Philosophers' saying: that salt, like vegetables, can be sown and multiplied.

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

### XXXIII. *A method showing the extraction of volatile and fixed Gold from the water in which minerals have been washed; and the profit that can be obtained from that water.*

This is the best method: Into a solution of Gold, or into the water which contains Gold, pour a solution of Silver <note>original: "Luna," the alchemical name for silver</note> or Lead <note>original: "Saturnus," the alchemical name for lead</note>, more or less, according to how much gold you suspect is present in that water. I propose an example.

Suppose there are 2 or 3 lots <note>a "lot" or "loto" was a historical unit of weight, roughly 1/32nd of a pound</note> of Gold in the water: Therefore, dissolve about 2 or 3 lots of Silver or Lead in Strong Water <note>original: "Aqua fortis," or nitric acid</note>, and pour this solution into the water containing the Gold, whether the quantity of it be large or small; mix everything together by vigorous stirring, so that the water takes on the appearance of milk: after everything has settled by resting, stir it again, and repeat this motion several times over the space of one hour, and finally allow everything to seek the bottom by resting. Separate all the clear water from the sediment by pouring it off, and strain the sediment itself through a filter, so that the water is completely separated from the Silver. This Silver must be dried and reduced back into its original body, in the manner which will soon follow.

**Note Well.** If the Silver or Lead has not extracted all the Gold from the water, which can easily happen, that gold is nevertheless [not] lost

### XXXIV. *Another and better method of eliciting Gold from Aqua Regia.*

Make an ash from the roasting, pour the Extract into an iron pot, and stir the Lime <note>The solid residue</note> with an iron spatula while it is cooked. All the acrid Spirits <note>Acidic vapors</note> adhere to it, and only the Phlegm <note>original: "Phlegma"; the watery, inactive component of a liquid</note> vanishes through evaporation. Therefore, when the Spirits are thoroughly concentrated and dried within, they are placed in a secret pot or crucible <note>original: "crucibulo"</note>; then the fire, receiving the concentrated Spirits into a vessel, drives them out, so that they can be used for a new extraction. The volatile Gold remains mixed with the Iron, which lime—reduced in a furnace suitable for this operation, which the Germans call a *Stichofen* <term>Stichofen: a type of blast furnace or 'tapping furnace' where molten metal is drained from the bottom</term>—brings forth Lead mixed with Gold. This, when driven off through the cupel <note>original: "cupellam"; a shallow porous cup used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead</note>, enriches the operators with the finest worked Gold and Silver.

### XXXV. *An easy preparation of Aqua Regia, for the extraction of Minerals.*

Because a copious multitude of Spirit of Salt <note>Hydrochloric acid</note> is easily prepared, one only needs to dissolve Saltpeter <note>Potassium nitrate</note> in it, and the Minerals are to be extracted with that solution. For the Saltpeter strengthens the Spirit of Salt so that it may more easily attack and seize the delicate Gold within those minerals. This same Spirit of Salt can be used for this operation even without any rectification <note>The process of refining a liquid by repeated distillation</note>, just as it rises in the first distillation.

### XXXVII. *How the Lime of Silver, which has fished the Gold out of the Royal Water, is to be recovered.*

### XXXVIII. *How precipitated Silver is to be reduced without loss of weight.*

Since this silver lime has been rendered highly volatile <note>original: "fugacitas"; meaning it easily evaporates or turns to smoke when heated</note>, so that its former body cannot be restored to it in common crucibles without great loss, this inconvenience cannot be met in a more suitable way than by the following method.

<margin>Lime</margin> Mix an equal weight [of flux] with this volatile lime, and place it into a glowing secret crucible—one that is narrower at the top and wider at the bottom. From this, nothing will escape in smoke, nor will even the smallest amount penetrate through the crucible; and all the Silver which has taken up the Gold is acquired in this way without any loss. You shall reduce this into grains by melting <note>original: "fusionem"</note>.

### XXXIX. *Another method of reducing fleeting Silver with greater utility.*

<margin>n 71</margin>Upon the helm of this Golden Little Ship <note>A metaphorical term for the alchemical vessel or the specific alloy being treated</note> place a small fish, named the Remora <note>The "suckerfish"; here used as a symbolic term for a chemical additive—likely a salt or mineral—intended to "stop" or fix volatile metals</note>, so that it may be stripped of its fleeting nature and become stable. What other advantages one might obtain by the help of this fleeting Silver, we shall hereafter teach, God willing.

### XL. *An operation by the "wet way" teaching how to extract Stones, and poor Ores of Silver and Copper.*

In the same manner, these materials are heated to a glow, extinguished in water, crushed in a mill, moistened with Strong Water <note>original: "Aqua forti"; nitric acid</note>, and extracted, just as was handed down above concerning the ores of Gold. No difference occurs here except for the extracting Waters, since Gold is extracted by Royal Water <note>original: "Aquam Regis"; nitro-muriatic acid</note>, and Silver is extracted by Strong Water. If Ores of Gold and Silver are available together, the Gold is extracted by Royal Water and the Silver by Strong Water, and the solutions are mixed; in which solution the Silver, precipitated by the Royal Water, simultaneously carries the Gold from the Royal Water down with it. And even if Copper was present in the Silver Ore, having been extracted along with the Silver by the Strong Water, it is in no way a hindrance to the operation itself. For the Silver and Gold seek the bottom, and the Copper is retained by the Royal Water, to be used afterwards for a new operation, and indeed as many times as one pleases. The Copper is recovered from the Royal Water by placing thin plates of iron into it, which operation renders the Royal Water red and entirely unfit for similar labors. Therefore

### XLI. *A method far easier still, without smelting, to extract Gold and Silver copiously from poor Ores, Sand, Clay, and other similar Minerals by means of Fire.*

Take the Ore, Clay, etc., containing either volatile or fixed Gold, which has been roasted and finely ground in a mill. Fill a glass gourd <note>a cucurbit or flask used in distillation</note>—either protected by a coating of lute <note>a clay sealant used to protect glass from high heat</note> or made of the finest earth—up to the halfway point. Pour enough of the following solvent <term>menstruum: a solvent used to dissolve solids, particularly in alchemy</term> over the material so that it is thoroughly moistened. As soon as that solvent is poured on, it immediately begins to give off smoke; therefore, it is absolutely necessary that you immediately place the Alembic <note>the "head" or cap of the still</note> upon the gourd. This must be placed in a sand bath within a cupel <note>a shallow vessel</note> so that all the moisture may be drawn out by distillation. While the distillation is occurring, the Gold is dissolved. The solvent itself, collected in the receiving vessel, retains the strength of Aqua Regia and may be used again for the same purpose, as will soon be described. After all the moisture has departed, remove the cooled gourd from the sand and pour a little water onto the material so that it softens and the Salt—in which the Gold contained in the ore lies hidden—may be extracted. Coagulate this lye <note>a liquid solution of salts</note> which is full of Gold into a red salt, which, after adding Litharge <note>lead monoxide</note>, is melted in such crucibles...

### XLV. *Another easier method for extracting Gold & Silver from Ores.*

Mix the Ore with the required Waters <note>likely mineral acids or "aqua fortis"</note>, and moisten it gradually; throw the whole mixture into a <unclear>retort</unclear>. The Spirits <note>volatile vapors or gases</note> pass out into a receiving vessel, and during the distillation, the Gold and Silver are dissolved by that solvent. Once extracted, they remain with the <unclear>dead head</unclear> <term>caput mortuum: the useless residue left in a flask after distillation</term> and are washed out from the Ore by water, so that they are acquired without great expense, and the Spirits collected in the receiving vessel compensate for all the costs of the operation.

### XLVI. *An easy Operation for extracting Gold and Silver abundantly from fatty Clay.*

Although Gold and Silver may be extracted from Ores through a troublesome operation using liquid Waters, as we taught at the beginning, nevertheless an Extraction of this kind brings no small profit, because the metals can be freed from the waters by precipitation, and the Waters themselves serve for the making of Saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder and further chemical solvents</note>. With fatty Clay, however, the situation is different, because the Spirit creeps into the fatty earth, and scarcely half the portion is recovered in the receiver unless the earth is first deprived of its fatness by heating it to a glow <note>calcination</note>, a process which also requires its own particular labor.

### XLVIII. *A most firm demonstration that, by the methods previously prescribed, sharp Waters and Salts elicit more Gold and Silver from Gold- and Silver-bearing Minerals—almost without expense—than costly smelting fires.*

To anyone endowed with even a moderate knowledge of gold-bearing minerals <term>Minerarum auriferarum; ores containing gold</term>, it is by no means unknown that gold can in no way be melted out of them without the addition of lead or other fusible materials. Therefore, when there is little gold present in the ores, and yet much lead or other materials must be added for the sake of easy melting, who will be equal to bearing such great expenses? Hence it has happened that poor minerals of this kind have been unable to contribute any utility to the common good.

Even assuming that there is enough gold and silver in the minerals to bear the costs of fire and smelting, and to supply gold and silver with profit: nevertheless, such operations hardly compare to my inventions—which use waters and salt—any more than water compares to wine, or night to day. For first, smelting far exceeds in its costs the extraction which is completed through salt and waters. Secondly, it can never happen that smelting expels all the gold and silver from the minerals without leaving something behind in the dross <term>scoriis; the slag or waste matter separated from metals during smelting</term>. Furthermore, there is a total loss of all "fugitive" <note>original: "fugacis"; referring to volatile metals that evaporate or are lost as vapor during high-heat processing</note> gold and silver, which by the for- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely "forti" or "fornacis" referring to the strength of the furnace.</note>

<summary>Glauber argues that his chemical solvent methods (using salts and acids) are far more cost-effective and efficient than traditional smelting, which requires expensive additives like lead and loses precious metal in the slag.</summary>

On the contrary, among the more ample and wealthy advantages which the extraction of Gold and Silver from Ores <note>original: "Mineris"</note> abundantly brings, this is not the least: that not only is all the fixed Gold and Silver extracted without any loss, but even those two metals which are still volatile <note>original: "fugacia," referring to "fleeting" or unstable elements that evaporate during heating</note> are attracted at the same time, and are rendered fixed and constant in the fire, whereas they would otherwise be utterly dissipated by the violence of the smelting fire and reduced to nothing. ...are fulfilled. These things which I say might be a stumbling block to many, who would perhaps say: in what way could it be possible that gold and silver might be torn from such common stones, which are fit only for paving the streets? To these I respond: it is indeed rare for fixed and constant gold and silver <note>original: "Aurum & Argentum fixum ac constans"; in alchemy, "fixed" refers to a substance that can withstand the heat of a furnace without vaporizing</note> to be found in such colored flints; nevertheless, they are always endowed with a spiritual, fleeting, and immature gold and silver. The salts, by extracting these, not only render them fixed and constant, but even reconcile a degree of gold to a small portion of lead <note>original: "Saturno"; the alchemical name for lead</note> which is added during the melting and separation. Therefore, in all places of the earth, wherever stones show themselves, anyone can be made a participant of good gold and silver without much labor or expense, and with great profit. For this most excellent discovery <note>original: "Inventione"</note>, let there be praise and eternal glory to God, the giver of all good things, *Amen.*

### XLIX. *A fundamental and evident demonstration, that the true Transmutation of Metals can be practiced in all places of the Earth, wherever men are able to dwell, even indeed in the smallest cottages of Peasants, and certainly with the same materials and vessels which are found within them.*

IN the preceding dissertations it has been sufficiently and evidently demonstrated, that the transmutation of Metals must be brought about through Salt and Fire. Therefore, because no man is permitted to pass his life without Salt and Fire, and these two requirements pertaining to the transmutation of Metals are ready at hand in all places, it necessarily follows, that the transmutation of Metals can be completed in all places, wherever mortals have fixed their habitations.

It is as well known as that which is most known, <note>A Latin idiom meaning "it is common knowledge."</note> that even the poorest and most lowly peasant [cannot live] without fire, nor salt, nor earthen vessels, for cooking

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

...necessary foods, he can do without. Therefore, once provided with salt, a copper fragment from some old bronze vessel will easily be available, which he will be entirely free to transmute into a better and nobler body through Fire and Salt.

However, because man has within himself a far better Salt, more convenient for the transmutation of Metals <note>Glauber is referring to the alchemical belief that the human body contains potent "microcosmic" reagents</note>, which excels common Salt in its quality, he rightly and deservedly employs his own Salt rather than one from an outside source, and does so in this manner:

Boil your own urine, or that of another person, to the consistency of honey; in this boiling, all the useless moisture of the Urine passes away through evaporation, and its Salt remains in the bronze kettle or pot. To this thickened Urine, mix in enough quicklime <term>quicklime: calcium oxide, a caustic alkaline substance</term> or ashes from burnt wood so that it turns into a thick mass. You should have ready copper plates cut into pieces and purified by heating them until they glow, along with an earthen pot having its own lid, into which that mass of Urine and quicklime may be placed along with the copper plates. When you have all these things ready, mix the Urine with the lime or ashes as was said, and fill your pot with this mixture to the height of three or four fingers' breadth; upon this material you shall place several copper plates, putting more material again over these, and so continuing to arrange these layers until you have filled the whole pot. With its lid...

One pound of this is to be valued more than two or three pounds of ordinary copper; for one pound of copper usually yields one pound and eight or ten lots <note>a "lot" was a small unit of weight, roughly 1/32nd of a pound</note> of this color, and thus it demonstrates a useful transmutation of copper.

Whoever desires to separate Gold and Silver from this color—of which large masses are not acquired, but only enough to demonstrate that the thing can be done—must use the following operation.

### LI. *In what way pure Gold can be drawn out of any Copper.*

In all copper, a spiritual gold lies hidden, which is not noticed during the labor of separation by means of lead in a <term>cupel</term> <note>A shallow, porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead</note>. This is nothing other than the color of the copper, adhering so firmly to its own body that it is extremely difficult to separate from it. Nevertheless, an operation rightly instituted—even if it is not profitable—demonstrates the possibility of the matter. For the sake of testing this, namely whether gold is present in all copper, one must work in the following manner.

This sediment, when dried and separated in a cupel <note>a shallow porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead</note>, will leave behind a small grain of Gold. Among the Vitriols, those from Hungary, Cyprus, India, Japan, and other similar ones excel, which are found in mines of gold-bearing copper and are treated in the following manner.

### LIII. *How gold is to be drawn out from sea salt or sea water; not indeed for the sake of profit, but merely so that it may be demonstrated that gold is hidden even in sea water or sea salt.*

Fill a large copper cauldron with sea water, and pour into it a little dissolved lead; this, seeking the bottom because it cannot endure the salt, is soon precipitated into a white powder. Frequently stir and agitate the water in the cauldron so that the lead solution can touch the sea water everywhere. By this action, the spiritual gold <note>original: "Spirituale Aurum"; likely referring to gold in a volatile or non-metallic state before precipitation</note> adheres to the lead powder and seeks the bottom with it. ### 54. *How Copper, as well as Gold itself (if present), may be easily and without great expense extracted and separated from poor Copper Ores, from which commonly no profit can be obtained.*

Gravelly or sulfurous Copper Ore must be roasted until the Sulfur is consumed; this is because sharp waters <note>Acidic solvents</note> do not attack sulfurous materials. Once the ore is roasted and crushed into a powder, fill a cucurbit <note>A gourd-shaped chemical vessel</note> and pour over it our Secret Solvent, which I taught above regarding the extraction of ores. By distilling it off, the entire solvent is recovered, and indeed not without an increase in its strength. The Copper and Silver, now dissolved, remain in the Salt which stayed in the Ore after the distillation; this must be washed out with water. From this water, which has absorbed that salt, the Gold contained within can be drawn out by means of a solution of lead or Silver. If, however, the same water is boiled down until a skin forms on the surface, it will harden into green Vitriol <note>Iron or copper sulfate crystals</note> when exposed to the cold. To remove the Copper itself from that salty water, iron rods are placed into it, which attract the copper; this is then scraped off, washed with water, and melted into copper through fusion, or

### LV. *How Gold may be separated from Copper with easy labor by means of Fire and Salt.*

IF Gold has been mixed with a mass of copper, all the copper must be reduced to ash by a glowing fire. This ash is then drenched with our Secret Vinegar <note>original: "Arcano Aceto"; likely a strong mineral acid or a specialized alchemical solvent</note>, which dissolves only the copper while leaving the Gold alone. Through boiling, the copper is dissolved, leaving behind the contained Gold like a shining powder. This must be dried and melted with Borax <term>Borax: a flux used in soldering and smelting to help metals flow and remove impurities</term>, from which Gold of 23 Carats is produced. That secret vinegar of ours withdraws all the copper from the Golden lime <note>original: "calci Aureæ"; the powdered residue of the metal after heating</note>. The copper is then separated from the Vinegar by means of inserted iron rods, as we taught in the preceding method.

THIS pure copper can be moistened with the strongest vinegar and placed in earthen pots. ### LVII.

**Note Well:** In extracting copper from ores, one must take account of this fact: namely, that sometimes Calamine stone <note>lapis Calaminaris; a zinc ore</note> or Zinc is found mixed into poor and stubborn copper ores or veins, which is in no way noticed in them. If, however, those ores are extracted with Royal Water <note>Aqua Regis; a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids</note>, and this is to be removed through distillation, no Royal Water comes out, but only a tasteless phlegm <note>an alchemical term for a watery, inactive distillate</note>. This happens because the Calamine stone or the Zinc retains all the sharpness within itself, just as if these two minerals said to the Royal Water: "We will not let you go yet, inasmuch as we still have need of your labor for our improvement," etc. 

It is certain, however, that whatever minerals and whatever metals retain sharp spirits <note>acidic vapors or liquids</note> within themselves are still immature, and they can be matured by spirits of this kind so that they may yield Gold and Silver, just as has already been said, and shall be said even more at length.

### 57. *How to profitably extract not only Lead, but also Gold and Silver, from wild Lead ores or veins that do not allow for smelting, and from which good lead—much less Gold or Silver—cannot be drawn out by common methods.*

Just as we stated above that some ores or veins of copper present themselves in the mines which, on account of the addition of Calamine stone <note>A zinc ore, historically used in making brass</note> or Zinc, reject all smelting and cannot be mastered by any fire; so also we advise here that Lead ores are found which indeed contain much Lead, but because of the Calamine stone, Zinc, and admixed sulfurous gravel, they cannot be overcome by any smelting. For these materials take away the easy flow from the Lead and cause such ores—which for the most part hide no small amount of Gold and Silver alongside the Lead—to be cast aside as utterly unfit and useless. Nevertheless, a great deal of profit might be obtained from them in this manner:

Let the ore be broken into very small pieces by pounding, and let it be roasted with glowing coals in my small **Secret Furnace**, which I have designed for roasting ores, so that the thick Sulfur may catch fire and burn away. If during the process the material should cluster together and form little balls by massing up, let it be removed from the furnace and pounded again,

Finally, once this ash has been washed, the dead and useless matter separates itself from the good and metallic earth; when this earth is melted by itself in a furnace—called a <term>Stichofen</term> <note>A "tapping furnace" used in smelting where the molten metal is periodically drained or "tapped" from the bottom.</note> by the Germans—it becomes molten lead, which contains gold and silver.

However, if the ore should be so wild <note>meaning "refractory" or resistant to processing</note> that it utterly rejects all melting and cannot be reduced by itself, yet nevertheless contains gold and silver, some litharge <note>original: "Lithargyrii"; lead oxide, often used as a flux to help metals melt</note> must be added to that metallic earth. This provides it with a flow and yields that gold-bearing and silver-bearing lead which, through common operations, completely refuses to present itself.

### 58. *Another way, teaching how to extract silver and gold by means of salt and fire, with great profit, from all wild and untamable metallic earths, in whose veins lead, copper, gravel, iron, or calamine stone <note>original: "lapis Calaminaris"; a zinc ore</note> have for the most part joined in company, and which deny all usefulness to common operations.*

Just as fire burns away all thick and combustible sulfur in ores, so that they may finally submit themselves to melting and [become] ductile metals

For that reason, such minerals—with the addition of common Salt, as was said above—must be roasted in the coals, so that the greedy, thick Sulfur <note>In early chemistry, sulfur was often viewed as the principle of combustibility and impurity in metals.</note> may vanish by burning away, and at the same time the Metal itself may be promoted toward maturity. In this way, good Gold and Silver may be separated from this very substance, whereas otherwise one would not have obtained even the slightest amount from these same minerals. Such improvement and change of imperfect Metals into more perfect ones can be acquired with the help of Salt and Fire. If, therefore, common Salt and thick Fire are able to bring this to effect in Metals, what might those uncommon, but Secret Fires of Salts <note>original: "Arcani Ignes Salium"; likely referring to concentrated acids or alchemical spirits</note> achieve in transmuted Metals that are already pure, making them even purer and more subtle?

### LIX. *How Metals are to be improved through pure Fires, or the Fiery Spirits of Salts.*

It can be felt with the hands <note>i.e., it is a tangible, demonstrable fact</note> that if thick Salt and Fire can confer something good upon thicker Metals, then surely a purer Fire and Salt will bring even more benefit to purer Metals. Just as instruments, whose edges are made sharper by sharpening—

<summary>This page explains the alchemical role of salt and fire in "ripening" base minerals into gold and silver by removing volatile impurities like sulfur. It suggests that if common materials can achieve this, "secret" alchemical salts can further refine already pure metals into even higher states.</summary>

rendered, they are far more suitable for working than those endowed with a duller edge, which accomplish less. The sharper the drill, the faster it pierces the wood; conversely, the duller it is, the more with difficulty it bores through. He who is eager to achieve something good in the <term>improvement of metals</term><note>Latin: emendatio metallorum; the process of refining or transmuting base metals into noble ones</note> must necessarily employ the subtle and strong <term>Fires of Salts</term><note>Glauber refers to concentrated acids or reactive saline solutions as "fires" due to their corrosive power</note>, so that by them he may destroy the metals, kill them, and lead them back to their former life, and thus provide them with more noble bodies. 

When their former body is restored to those moist and cold Fires of Salts—namely, so that they return to the form of a salt, but one more noble and subtle—the metals can be destroyed, killed, and recalled to life far more swiftly by them, because a double fire accomplishes more than a single one. Since, therefore, Salt by itself is nothing other than pure and concentrated fire, and the fire of wood or coals joins itself with it, a much greater efficacy must altogether be expected from them than from common fire alone. 

However, we have mentioned such operations until now as being not necessary to be repeated here. It will be permitted for every Chemist to gather and learn from what has been said so far those things which concern the improvement of metals that stand in need of the help of Salt and Fire. More shall follow, God willing.

# First Century.

### LX. *Let us now ascend higher and demonstrate what incredible miracles our Secret Fires of Salts perform within that Great Work of the Philosophers.*

Just as it has been more than sufficiently established in the preceding pages that immature metals can be promoted to a more perfect maturity in a particular way <note>The author refers to "particular" transmutation, which affects only a portion of the material, as opposed to "universal" transmutation</note> by the aid of Fire and Salt: so also is this clearly evident in the multiplication of Animals and Vegetables. For instance, if sufficient food and drink are provided to an infant, it grows daily in size and bodily strength until it reaches perfection and its manly age. The same Multiplication presents itself to our sight in Vegetables, in which a tiny seed or a small root draws its nourishment from the Salt of the Earth and the Solar rays, rising up into a perfect, large, and fertile tree. This particular transformation is before everyone's eyes and is therefore very well known. However, in what manner the most noble part, or the purest essence <note>original: "effentia"</note>, is to be extracted from the bodies of Vegetables, Minerals, and Animals—so that other weaker bodies may be strengthened and improved by it—the Philosophers have always hidden and concealed with the greatest zeal. Hence there have been very few, and even now they are rare, who have had or still have knowledge of this highest science. Of the babblers...

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

Indeed, there is everywhere a great abundance of those who babble about hundreds of arts with their mouths, yet in reality know not how to perform even the smallest feat, as is clearly apparent from the vain chatter of I. H. S. <note>Likely a reference to a specific contemporary rival or critic of Glauber; the initials were often used in polemical scientific debates of the 17th century.</note> Consequently, matters have progressed to the point where almost no one is willing to believe that the true transmutation of metals <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into gold or silver.</note> exists in the nature of things.

<margin>+</margin> Therefore, as far as the <term>Great Work</term> <note>The "Opus Magnum," the ultimate goal of alchemy to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of the Philosophers is concerned, all Philosophers unanimously assert in their writings—which I also affirm as most true in all my own writings, pointing it out as if with my very fingers—that nothing in the nature of things can produce a tincture <note>A substance capable of changing the color and quality of a metal.</note> or dye with a painting of the most pleasing color, except for one single <term>Sulphur</term>. <note>In alchemy, "Sulphur" refers to the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter, not just the chemical element.</note> And this same combustible, immature, and fleeting substance is fixed and changed into a true tincture by the operation and help of Salts. This is as certain as anything can be, and yet it is also laborious and requires a sufficiently long space of time, especially if one persists in the <term>wet way</term>. <note>The "via humida," a slower alchemical process involving liquid solutions and gentle heat, contrasted with the "dry way" using high-heat crucibles.</note> The path to reaching the end of such a work, in my opinion—saving the judgment of others—would be best if one were to select a Sulphur of the kind that has already been brought to perfect maturity by nature; this would bring the advantage of not requiring a longer time for its own maturation.

However, such a fixed and tinging Sulphur is found nowhere more readily than in metals, and especially in copper and iron, but it is found better and more purely in Gold,

# First Century

The discovery of this [substance], however, has always been considered almost impossible, as has its separation, because of its very firm and intimate union with its own body. For researchers have generally lacked a true separator everywhere who could segregate the purer part from the more impure. For, as is well known, a hard and compact metallic body of this kind can only be segregated and divided into its parts with great difficulty. 

Dissolution by means of <term>Sharp Waters</term><note>original: "Acrium Aquarum"; likely referring to strong mineral acids like nitric acid or aqua regia</note> certainly absorbs every metal, but it effects no separation. Because metals are homogeneous, and the <term>Metallic Sulfur</term><note>The active, combustible principle in alchemy</note> is so tightly bound to its <term>Metallic Mercury</term><note>The fluid, passive principle in alchemy</note> through the bond of the <term>Metallic Salt</term><note>The stabilizing or solidifying principle; together these three form the "Tria Prima"</note>, it can never happen that one part is severed from the other out of such solutions, whether through precipitation or through other means. 

If a dissolved metal is precipitated out of the Water, all the parts fall out again just as mixed as they were before the solution, and they allow for no separation. If, however, one wished to make the metals <insert>simultaneously</insert> spiritual, so that the purer parts might be disjoined from the more impure through distillation, no separation occurs even then; instead, the body itself ascends in a purer form and again consists of its three principles just as before. In this state, it is indeed more effective in <term>Alchemy</term> and <term>Medicine</term> than the coarser bodies of metals, but it remains unfit for the true <term>tincture</term><note>The "tincture" refers to the elusive substance capable of transmuting metals or curing all diseases</note>, since in all bodies nothing works except the living, and other...

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

The soul is that which brings dead bodies to life. For it is the Spirit, as Christ himself says, that gives life; the body is of no use <note>original: "Spiritus est... qui vivificat; Corpus inutile est." A reference to John 6:63.</note>. Let man be an example, or any other animal, which lives, moves, and acts for as long as the Spirit—the Author of life—is present within it. However, when that Spirit vanishes, the body lacks all motion and becomes a corpse. Now, if it were possible for us to capture such animal Spirits and render them into physical form, we would perform incredible feats with them, and perhaps even fashion a living animal from a clod of earth—a power which God, however, has reserved for Himself alone. 

Yet, that kind of Father has granted this to us: that we may extract pure souls or essences from immobile subjects (those lacking animal life and movement) and make them physical, and with them achieve things of great importance in Medicine and Alchemy. Among these, the Metallic Souls <note>The "soul" of a metal usually refers to its essential color or its combustible principle, often identified as "Sulphur."</note> excel; they are indeed more fixed and constant than the essences of Vegetables, but they are far more difficult to acquire. For the souls of Vegetables allow themselves to be extracted quite easily; but the colors and souls of Metals admit extraction only with difficulty. For that reason, the ignorant crowd considers such a thing to be something that could never, at any time, be accomplished. And not without reason, since the separation of the <term>Tinging Soul</term> <note>Tingentis Animæ: The concentrated essence capable of "tinging" or transforming the color and quality of a base metal into gold or silver.</note> from a hard Metallic body is a matter of great difficulty. 

Many ways of acquiring this <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur: In alchemy, this is not just the yellow mineral, but the active, masculine, and combustible principle within all matter.</note> have been sought, and regarding its acquisition, several "Brothers of Ignorance" and slanderers have published books,

Nevertheless, a few things must be briefly advised here: that like attracts and extracts its like. If something **Mercurial** <note>The principle of fluidity and metallic luster</note> is to be drawn out from metallic masses, a Mercurial solvent must be employed, since like rejoices in its like. Thus, **Sulphurous** essences wish to be drawn out by Sulphurous solvents, and not by Mercurial ones, because waters most willingly associate with waters, and oils with oils. Therefore, because all the Philosophers write that the Sulphur, or the tinging Soul in **Iron** and **Copper**, corresponds in goodness and nobility to the Sulphur in **Gold**, in my judgment it would not be necessary to use Gold, but only to lead Iron and Copper to the point where they become transparent glasses from which the colors could be extracted. If, however, anyone should prefer Gold to these and wish to elicit a **Tincture** <note>A potent medicinal or transformative extract</note> from it, he is entirely free to do so, and will find in many...

It is truly a secret of the greatest importance to possess the skill of preparing such a solvent, which penetrates the hardest stones and the densest glasses, extracts from them, and likewise bestows various colors upon others, without any corruption, breaking, or destruction of the body. While it seems likely to me that this is true, the method remains as yet unknown. ### LXI. *How subtle Vegetable Sulfur is to be sharpened by Nitrous and moist Fire, so that it may extract the fixed Sulfur of Metals, or the pure coloring Soul.*

First, let the **Oils of Vegetables** be brought to the highest degree of purity and subtlety through frequently repeated distillations and rectifications. Afterward, let them be rectified once more by means of some concentrated **Nitrous Fire** <note>likely a reference to nitric acid or a derivative of saltpeter</note>. In this way, these oils—already subtle in themselves—acquire a fiery vigor and the faculty endowed with the power of attacking **Metallic Sulfurs** and extracting them from bodies that are hard and so tightly compacted. For any Vegetable Oil, no matter how subtly prepared, lacks the power of entering Metals, much less their glasses, unless it is first—

# First Century

clarified bodies, as they are called by the Chemists <note>original: "Chymicis"; referring to early modern alchemists and laboratory practitioners</note>, differ among themselves.

Clarified bodies, therefore, are nothing other than bodies purged and cleansed by the operation of Fire. For Fire is the ultimate examiner of all things, being powerful in that strength by which it burns all things, reduces them to ashes and dust, and from the ash produces glass—which is surely that final state into which all things are reduced. If, therefore, there is anything of good within a subject which, when burned by Fire, is changed into transparent glass through fusion, it manifests itself in that final essence of its own and shines forth with its own brilliance, so that anyone may discern what lay hidden in its life before the combustion. For example: by burning wood, or a herb, or an animal, I transform it into ashes, and by the help of fusion I convert these very ashes into glass. Once this is done, no color comes into view, since the glass remains white, because the Sulfur <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the principle of combustibility and soul</note> has been consumed by the combustion, and the Mercury <note>Mercury represents the principle of fluidity and spirit</note> has flown away into the air as smoke, seeing as these are two principles which are in no way able to resist the forces of Fire. But the Salt <note>Salt represents the principle of fixity and the physical body</note>, a scorner of Fire, remained in the earth as a thing of no efficacy.

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

For such metallic glasses are tinted with color according to the nature and property of the metal, which it kept hidden before its combustion <note>In early chemistry, "combustion" or "calcination" referred to heating a metal until it turned into a powder or ash.</note>. We shall clarify the matter with an example.

If I combust copper or iron, reducing them to ashes, and convert them into glass by melting, the copper by itself—without the addition of any other thing—will produce a green glass, and the iron will show a glass of a yellow color. However, with the addition of other substances, different colored glasses will come forth. For example:

If I add lead glass to combusted iron, or <term>Crocus Martis</term> <note>original: "Crocus Martis"; literally "Saffron of Mars," a red iron oxide powder used by alchemists.</note>, the glass produced through melting will have the yellow color of a Hyacinth stone. This same <term>Crocus Martis</term>, when melted with common glass made from wood ashes and salt, will give a glass of a greenish color, which is the natural and proper color of iron. For the yellow color of the former glass made from iron was caused by the lead added to the iron during the melting, which prevented the iron from being able to manifest its own true and natural color. Glasses of two colors melted together display false colors, just as is apparent from blue and yellow glass, which when melted together show a green color both outside the fire and within the fire itself. Hence, the occasion was given to me for writing and teaching the method of investigating, by means of melted glasses, what kind of metal is hidden in an ore or in some metallic earth. This method of testing ores is far better and swifter than that which is done in a <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "cupella"; a shallow, porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead.</note> through boiling,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of metallurgical experiments and the extraction of "internal colors" from metals through vitrification.</meta>

# First Century

...and the exhalation of lead, as is commonly done. Thus, take 5, 6, 8, or 10 grains of any <term>ore</term><note>original: "Mineræ"</note> crushed into powder, and mix in one or two <term>lots</term><note>A historical unit of weight, roughly half an ounce</note> of <term>Venetian glass</term><note>original: "vitri Venetici"; a high-quality, easily melted glass famous in the Renaissance</note> that melts easily. Place the mixed material in a well-covered <term>crucible</term><note>original: "crucibulo"; a ceramic or graphite container used for high-temperature melting</note> and reduce it into glass by melting. Whatever metal the ore contained will present its color to the eyes within the glass.

Thus, **lead** will appear in a grey color, **tin** in white, **copper** in sea-green, **iron** in a greenish hue, **silver** in yellow, and **gold** in a blue color. Each of these colors exists as the true and internal color of its respective metal. Gold can indeed imitate the **ruby** with its color if other colors are added to it; nevertheless, the blue color is its own proper and natural color, just as yellow is for silver. This is most consistent with the truth, although it may seem strange to the ignorant, who know only external appearances but are utterly ignorant of internal natures.

However, the colors of gold and silver become better and more perfectly known if a bit of fixed and white **sulfur** is added to them, which prevents the gold and silver from being fully reduced back into their metallic bodies during the melting. If a <term>calx</term><note>A powder produced by heating a metal; an oxide</note> of gold or silver is melted with added **borax**, the gold and silver are restored to their original bodies and do not pass into a glass imbued with any color. But if these same powders are melted with a glass of easy fusion to which a little crushed **flint** has been added, the flints, by means of their own sulfur, retain the gold and silver with them, so that in their whole...

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 111: Glauber continues his discussion on the coloring of glass using metallic ores and the revelation of hidden metallic properties through fusion.</meta>

->Joh. Rud. Glauber<-

do not admit fusion into a [metallic] body, but remain in the glass with some part of themselves, which renders their internal colors visible—colors that otherwise would never be exposed to sight.

**NB.** <note>Latin: Nota Bene; "Note well"</note> If ores of Gold and Silver were at hand and fused with glass, their colors would also appear, because there is always some Sulfur <note>In 17th-century chemistry, "sulfur" often referred to the principle of combustibility or a specific mineral impurity</note> present in ores, which prevents the whole metal from passing into a solid body; instead, by remaining in the glass with some part of itself, it manifests its color. It should also be noticed here that if by chance some ore or metallic earth contains not one, but two or more metals, that metal of which more is present than the others will shine forth in the glass after fusion above the rest. For example:

When about to make a trial with red Garnets, I take 8 or 10 grains converted into powder, mix them with a half-ounce <term>lotonem</term> <note>A "loto" or "loth" was a historical unit of weight, roughly 1/32 of a pound</note> of well-ground white Venetian glass, and by melting it, I change it into glass. In this transformation, not a red glass, but one of a very beautiful grass-green color will emerge; this will teach me which metals were hidden in those Garnets—namely, copper and iron, and indeed more of the latter than the former. And although some Gold might also have been present, it is nevertheless not noticed because of the iron, which predominates over the copper and Gold; for in this operation, only that metal presents itself to the sight which excels the others in its abundance.

<summary>Johann Rudolf Glauber explains how the hidden metallic content of minerals, such as garnets, can be identified by the specific colors they tint glass during fusion. He notes that the most abundant metal in a sample will determine the final color, such as iron producing a green tint even in red stones.</summary>

<keywords>Johann Rudolf Glauber, alchemy, glassmaking, metallic ores, gold, silver, sulfur, garnets, Venetian glass, copper, iron, chemical analysis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and alchemical discourse regarding the spiritual significance of material transformations, specifically focusing on the metaphor of glass-making as a symbol for the resurrection.</meta>

# First Century

By this process of vitrification <note>The transformation of a substance into glass through intense heat.</note>, Isaac Hollandus <note>A legendary 15th-century Dutch alchemist often cited in later Hermetic works.</note> intended to signify that after this life—specifically when the world has been consumed by fire—other clarified bodies shall emerge from the bodies of men reduced to ashes. These shall be tinged with such colors as their souls, whether good or evil, wove into their coarse bodies during this life. For what are beautiful colors other than the virtues of those subjects from which they emit their brilliance? With what glory, with what splendor shall the illustrious bodies of the godly shine, which shall surpass Rubies and other precious gems with their bright radiance? How dark, by contrast, will be the bodies of the ungodly, whose foulness and infamy shall be made plain to the whole world.

O Farner <note>Likely referring to Friedrich Farner, a contemporary critic or rival alchemist.</note> and Harprecht <note>Johann Harprecht, a 17th-century jurist and alchemist who wrote under the name 'Fama Aurea'.</note>, with what stinking obscurity will you one day appear before the Divine tribunal, along with your most foul followers and those fleeting, poison-filled Arsenical Ores <note>original: "Mineris Arsenicalibus"; a metaphor for volatile, deceptive, or toxic alchemical theories.</note>? Unless you depart from your malice and return to a fruitful state through repentance. At that time, it will become known to the whole world what lying spirits, prone to calumny, dwelt within your bodies. Whatever kind of soul held its domicile in the body of a man living and dying in this life, such a soul the Fire—as the ultimate Examiner of all things—shall at last manifest.

<summary>The author uses the alchemical process of vitrification to illustrate the resurrection of the dead, arguing that the moral quality of one's soul will determine the "color" and brilliance of their eternal body. He specifically warns his rivals, Farner and Harprecht, that their deceptive practices will be revealed by the divine fire.</summary>

<keywords>Isaac Hollandus, vitrification, resurrection, alchemy, Farner, Harprecht, Arsenical Ores, Divine Judgment, spiritual colors</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues Johann Rudolf Glauber's alchemical-theological discourse, moving from the physical properties of minerals to a moral exhortation based on the process of roasting ores.</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

O impious men, remove the foul, superfluous, and burning Sulfur from your hearts in this life by the roasting of a small spark, before that great Fire <note>A reference to the Final Judgment or the "Great Work" of spiritual purification</note> seizes you; and when the time for removing that malice has passed, may it not turn your bodies—with that most foul Sulfur adhering to them—into the blackest and most base dross <term>scoriae: the waste matter or slag left after smelting metal</term>.

Let a comparison be drawn from the smelting of minerals. In these, although a mineral of silver or copper may abound with much silver or gold, if the superfluous Sulfur were not removed before smelting by a small fire of roasting, but the mineral itself, with that thick Sulfur, were committed to the vehement fire of the furnace, no metal would indeed be obtained. Instead, that foul Sulfur would transmute the good metal into black dross. So also, no beautiful and transparent glass could be made from pure metals by melting if thick Sulfur of this kind were adhering to it.

Therefore, just as thick, earthy, foul, and rapacious Sulfur—if not removed from minerals and metallic earths by a small spark before smelting—attacks the good metal during the melting process and transmutes it into black dross; so also must men prepare themselves in this life, before death, for that universal Fire that will be present at the Last Judgment. They must remove their burning and superfluous Sulfur—namely, their sins and vices—from their hearts by the fire of repentance.

<summary>Glauber uses the metallurgical process of roasting sulfur out of ore as a metaphor for spiritual purification, warning that unless sins are removed through repentance in this life, the soul will be corrupted like metal turned to slag.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, metallurgy, sulfur, purification, repentance, Last Judgment, smelting, slag, morality</keywords>

# First Century

From the rapacity of that very one <note>Likely referring to the devil or the spirit of greed</note> still clinging to the hearts of those whom he has seized, they shall be deprived of even that small amount of good—if any still remains—in that universal Fire of the Last Judgment, and they shall be changed into horrendous dross. For a tiny amount of wild Sulfur can burn much good Metal into dross, just as a single mortal sin, with which a man is burdened when he passes away, is accustomed to plunge the body and soul of man into the abyss and the sulfurous pit of eternal death; so the ancient Church has always believed, and to this very opinion it has constantly adhered.

I could not pass over these few things concerning clarified bodies in silence; much less could I omit this: that the bodies of all things can be transformed into clarified bodies much better through our Secret Fire <term>Arcanum Ignem: a specialized alchemical heat, often chemical or philosophical rather than a literal flame</term> than through common fire. For the common fire puts to flight and expels the fleeting parts <note>original: "fugaces partes," referring to volatile elements like Mercury</note>, whereas on the contrary, our Fire preserves them, and together with the rest, renders them clear and fixed. For this reason, it is necessary that those bodies shine forth with such beautiful and splendid colors, surpassing those others in which common fire has expelled the Mercury and Sulfur, and left nothing remaining but the Salt.

How, moreover, such a transmutation into ashes is to be carried out through our moist and Secret Fires can be grasped by anyone with a bit of conjecture. For whatever is placed upon them must be burned into ashes, and indeed, ashes of a far different kind,

<summary>The author compares the spiritual purification of the soul at the Last Judgment to the alchemical clarification of matter, arguing that the "Secret Fire" of alchemy is superior to common fire because it preserves volatile essences.</summary>
<keywords>Last Judgment, Secret Fire, Sulfur, Mercury, Salt, Transmutation, Clarification, Alchemical Theory</keywords>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

others, than those which the combustions of common fire produce. For when any herb or wood is burned into ashes by common fire, the <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the principle of combustibility and soul</note> is consumed by the flame, the <term>Mercury</term> <note>Mercury represents the principle of volatility and spirit</note> flies away trusting in its wings, and the <term>Salt</term> <note>Salt represents the principle of stability and the physical body</note> remains in a little ash or a little earth. Our <term>philosophical calcination</term> <note>original: "calcinatio Philosophicæ"; a specialized heating process intended to purify rather than destroy</note> takes nothing away, but preserves all things together, first bringing a black coal into view, afterwards presenting colors of various beauty to the eyes, which it replaces with white, and finally completes this with a red, molten, and <term>Medicinal stone</term> <note>A reference to the Philosopher's Stone in its role as a universal healer</note>.

**NB.** <note>Latin abbreviation for "Note Well"</note> Here it must be properly noted, that in order to prepare a pure Medicine, a pure subject must also be employed. For if anyone should attempt to change any herb, wood, or animal into a Medicament by the power of the <term>Secret Fires</term> <note>original: "Secretorum Ignium"; likely referring to chemical catalysts or specific regulated heat</note>, all the ashes and dregs which were present in the herb would also adhere to the Medicament and render it impure. Necessity therefore demands that the whole herb or the whole animal not be taken, but only their <term>essential Salt</term>, which, being free from dregs, consists only of the pure principles of the herb; it allows itself to be converted into a red, tinging stone that easily admits solution more readily than the herb itself with its own dregs.

Indeed, I would wish that these few things handed down by me in this place not be considered of little importance. For they are such things which, under their veil, [cover] matters of no small weight

# First Century

They conceal [their secrets] with their own cheap covering, and do not wish to insinuate themselves into everyone's knowledge. This way of transmuting all things into clarified bodies, without the loss of their own weight, is the best of all. Indeed, those very bodies, clarified in this manner, are without doubt of greater efficacy than the coarse bodies of Animals, Vegetables, and Minerals themselves, which still swell with their thick and impure dregs.

If, however, anyone were not content with these things and aspired to greater heights, it would be advisable for him to extract the **Soul** <note>original: "Animam." In alchemy, the 'soul' refers to the essential quintessence or the most active internal principle of a substance.</note> from this red and fixed stone, and by a repeated operation, reduce it back to the form of a stone; by which, without a doubt, he would produce far greater effects. Moreover, the more frequently one repeats such an operation, the more effective a medicine he would obtain, with the medicine acquiring individual increases of its powers with each repetition, while through such actions the powers and virtues of things are driven into a narrow space and concentrated, by which wonderful things can be performed.

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

And these things, which have been said on this occasion regarding the reduction of all things into clarified bodies, the extraction of Souls from them, and the concentration of those same Souls into purer bodies, shall suffice. He who possesses a head so like a ram's <note>original: "caput vervecinum"; a common Latin idiom for someone who is dull-witted or stubborn</note> that he can grasp nothing at all from these instructions—I do not see by what means he might be helped.

Furthermore, we must see what Spirits are, and especially what concentrated Spirits are, and what they are capable of performing.

Since the subject matter at this point offers an opportunity to speak of Spirits, it must be explained in a few words.

### LXII. *What Spirits are, and in what manner they work good or evil.*

First, they are called Vegetable Spirits when Vegetables are crushed, and once crushed, are moistened with water (if they do not abound in their own juice or lack it entirely), and are thus brought to fermentation. After fermentation, they are distilled; this distillation produces subtle and effective Spirits which, beyond Medicine, are the producers of many useful things in Alchemy and other Arts.

Secondly, various and diverse Spirits are also prepared from Animals through distillation, such as from Blood, Urine, Hair, Horns, Hooves, and other similar parts of Animals, which <note>The text breaks off here, continuing on the next page with the properties of animal-based spirits.</note>

all Spirits have their use in Medicine and Alchemy.

Thirdly, Spirits are also driven out by Fire from Minerals and Metals, and especially from Salts, such as Vitriol <note>Iron or copper sulfate, used as a basis for many chemical reactions</note>, Alum, Saltpeter, common Salt, and other similar substances. The books of the Chemists are full of the preparations of these, which bids us to refrain from superfluous repetitions. However, their concentrations and uses have already been described by us previously.

It lies within the power of man to prepare all the aforementioned Spirits for his own use, and to accomplish things of great importance with them. Without these, little benefit or utility will be perceived in either Medicine or Alchemy, and not much good will be performed, even though they are of a fleeting nature and cannot endure the Fire. If, however, Art <note>The "Art" refers to the skill and technique of the alchemist</note> could bestow upon them constancy in the Fire, they would undoubtedly become the workers of far greater effects.

I here offer an opportunity for careful meditation to all diligent seekers of true Medicine and Alchemy: to weigh what might be achieved in Medicine and Alchemy if those fugitive Spirits were deprived of their flightiness and rendered constant by our fixing and "moist fires" <note>A term for chemical agents or solvents that stabilize volatile substances without separating their parts</note>, which do not segregate one part from another, but fix them all together as one. These are the few things we wished to say concerning the Spirits subjected to human power and serving the use of mortals.

Concerning other Spirits, whom God [has appointed] for his ministry...

As for the cause for which He created them—namely for the holy Angels and similar beings—we say nothing here, because what must be said about these belongs to another place: specifically, to my little book in which God and all mobile Spirits <note>original: "Spiritibus," referring here to non-corporeal entities or vital forces</note> will be treated. For here, I wished only to treat briefly the Spirits of Vegetables, Animals, and Minerals, insofar as they can be prepared for human uses.

### LXIII. *The Particular Use of Concentrated Spirits from Salts in Medicine.*

We have heard that concentrated Spirits, or the "moist Fires" of Salts, reduce all things Philosophically <note>In alchemy, "philosophically" refers to processes using chemical principles rather than brute force</note> into a lime <note>original: "calcem," a powder or residue left after heating</note>, without the expulsion of Mercury or the combustion of Sulfur. They do this so effectively that by preserving, altering, and improving all things, they fix them <note>To "fix" a substance meant to make it stable and resistant to heat</note>—something which common fire is unable to provide. From this, anyone can easily conjecture what excellent Medicines can be prepared with the aid of such great Fires. And because such excellent Medicines have been given to us by God for the sake of the human race, to be applied for common use, it would surely be impious to wrap them in silence and deprive wretched sick people of them.

What, I ask, is more miserable and full of sorrow in this world than a sick man, afflicted by the ineffable torture of the most vehement pains?

<summary>Glauber distinguishes between celestial spirits and the chemical spirits of nature, arguing that concentrated salt "fires" are divine gifts essential for creating superior medicines to relieve human suffering.</summary>
