<meta>This page continues the author's discourse on medical preparations, transitioning from a philosophical reflection on poverty and wealth to practical laboratory instructions.</meta>

# First Century

Even if a man possessed all the wealth of the whole world, he would nonetheless be more afflicted than any most-impoverished beggar <note>The author suggests that physical health is the true measure of wealth; without it, even a king is poorer than a healthy beggar</note>. Therefore, driven by love for my neighbor, I have determined to set down in this place several Medicines, both universal and particular—yet in such a way that they do not fall into the hands of unworthy Enemies, but are reserved for friends alone.

And first, indeed, a Medicine which corrects weak digestion of the Stomach.

Take: Teeth snatched from a wolf or a dog while still half-alive, after it has been struck down by a gunshot. Pour over them two or three parts of concentrated Spirit of Salt <note>original: "Spiritus Salis"; this is hydrochloric acid</note> in a gourd-shaped vessel <note>original: "cucurbita"; a rounded glass laboratory flask</note> placed in a sand bath to be heated with the proper fire, so that the oil of Salt may dissolve them and reduce them into a thick paste. From this pulp, all the sharpness of the Spirit of Salt must be drawn out by pouring on hot water, until nothing but a white paste remains. Since some oil of Salt still remains in this—as it does not allow itself to be entirely washed away by water—an equal portion of sugar candy should be mixed in, by which that sharpness may be broken and the paste prepared for a more pleasant use.

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

### LXIV. *An Antidote against Poison.*

The teeth of a rabid dog, prepared in the aforementioned manner <note>Glauber refers to his method of dissolving or softening hard animal matter using "Spirit of Salt" (hydrochloric acid)</note>, provide an antidote against poison. Likewise, the teeth and horns of all animals—but especially those of stags—resist all poisons after such preparation.

### LXV. *Which Animals' Teeth and Horns Excel Others in Medicine.*

The teeth and horns of all predatory animals, as well as those of sea monsters, are of great use in medicine; such as those of wolves, bears, lynxes, tigers, lions, leopards, and, in the water, crocodiles and similar predatory fish. Their horns, teeth, and scales—along with the talons of birds of prey—can be converted into good medicines by the concentrated Spirit of Salt <note>original: "Spiritum Salis"; a strong acid solution</note> in the manner described.

### LXVI. *An Experiment Indicating Which Worms Are Suitable for Medicine.*

Take a strong and properly rectified Spirit of Salt, or simply an effective *Aqua Fortis* <note>original: "Aqua fortis"; literally "strong water," usually referring to nitric acid</note>, and immerse in it a worm of any kind. The worm will attempt to escape by flight as soon as it feels that "moist fire." Because

### LXVII. *An experiment indicating which herbs are useful or useless for Medicine.*

Use the method indicated for the worms, and place the herbs—those you do not know or whose powers are hidden from you—one after another into the "moist fire" <note>A term for a chemical bath or slow-acting solvent</note>. That which dissolves more slowly surpasses in strength and virtue that which admits dissolution more quickly. For example: Lettuce, Purslane, Melons, Cucumbers, and similar watery herbs, along with fruits having a moist nature, are soon converted into water by those fires. Rosemary, sage, thyme, wild thyme, and other hotter herbs require a longer time for their dissolution. Ginger, Pepper, Clove, Nutmeg, Cinnamon, Cardamom, Zedoary <note>A root similar to ginger</note>, etc., draw out a longer delay in dissolution before they pass entirely into water. From this, the nature and properties of herbs become known to anyone with little trouble. It is also added that a Medicine is made so much more effective from a worm or any herb,

### LXVIII. *The method of preparing an effective Medicine from poisonous Worms and Insects.*

In the Second Part of the *Spagyric Pharmacopoeia* <note>Glauber’s famous work on "spagyric" or alchemical medicine, which focuses on separating and recombining the essential parts of plants and minerals</note>, I taught the method of correcting venomous insects through a liquor of fixed Nitre <note>Potassium carbonate, often used as a flux or solvent in alchemy</note>, and of changing their poison into an effective Medicine; the student of good medicines will find that there. In this place, however, it is taught in what manner such worms, and herbs abounding in poison, may be corrected by the concentrated Fire of Salt <note>Glauber’s term for a highly concentrated hydrochloric acid or a similar potent saline solvent</note>, and changed into excellent and penetrating Medicines: the operation is as follows.

1. Pour 2 or 3 ounces of our concentrated Fire of Salt into a glass vessel, into which you shall cast the worms from which the Medicine is to be prepared, one after another; however, do not add more than the said Fire is able to consume by dissolving. After they have all been dissolved and turned into water, having lost all poison, they have emerged as good Medicines.

<summary>Johann Rudolf Glauber describes a method for neutralizing the toxins in venomous insects and worms using a concentrated saline solvent to create potent medicinal remedies. He references his earlier work on "fixed nitre" while introducing this alternative chemical process using "Fire of Salt."</summary>

### LXIX. A Medicine from Worms, Dissolved by Moist Fires, and the Method of Separation.

A great distinction is found between worms and venomous insects. For they are partly dry, partly moist, and partly indeed of a fatty and oily nature and property; thus it is entirely necessary to distinguish their natures with the proper discernment. Insects that have a dry property, such as Spanish Flies <note>original: "Cantharides"; these beetles were historically used as a rubefacient or "blistering agent"</note> and similar creatures, are to be used in the form of a Salt. Watery worms, such as Earthworms, Spiders, and the like, provide their medicine in the form of a liquid. Balsamic worms, such as May-worms <note>original: "Majales vermes"; likely the Oil Beetle (Meloe majalis), known for secreting an oily substance</note> and others of that kind, besides the medicine they bestow, also yield a fatty and balsamic oil; this is much more effective than the liquid itself for both external and internal use in Medicine. So that the matter may be better understood, I shall set down the operation, which everyone will be able to follow as a guiding star <note>original: "Cynosuram"; literally the constellation Ursa Minor, used metaphorically as a guide</note>.

### LXX. The Operation to be Used in Treating All Kinds of Worms.

Take several ounces of May-worms, place them in a glass vessel, and pour on enough concentrated Spirit of Salt <note>term: Spirit of Salt (hydrochloric acid)</note> so that the worms are thoroughly covered and gradually dissolved. After they are completely dissolved

<meta>catchword: dissolved</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber
## Section LXXI.

Once they have been dissolved, pour the solution into a separating glass <note>original: "vitrum separatorium"; a vessel used in early chemistry to separate liquids of different densities, similar to a modern separatory funnel</note>. Close the opening of the glass with your finger, and hold the inverted glass closed with your finger until all the fatty Oil floats on top of the liquid. Remove your finger so that the liquid can flow out, and as the oil itself follows the escaping liquid, retain it by closing the opening again with your finger, so that it may be poured into another glass. Keep this Oil, or balsam, as a precious treasure, with which you will perform wonders in the cure of diseases, especially indeed for Gout <note>original: "Podagra"; a painful form of arthritis often affecting the big toe</note> and the Stone <note>original: "calculus"; referring to kidney or bladder stones</note>. 

From these worms, however, you will elicit only a little Oil; and when casting them into the dissolving liquid, you must take care not to touch them with your hands, but rather drop them into the glass having grasped them with small forceps. For they have this property: if they are touched by hands, they immediately stain them by releasing their fatty balsam, which smells almost like Musk <note>original: "Moschum"; a strong-smelling substance used in perfumes and medicine</note>. It is almost as if they were saying: "Grant us our life, we pray; all that we have, we will give to you: take this balsam, and use the same to cure incurable diseases."

Some men who are students of such matters have collected this balsam and found it to be far more efficacious than the worms themselves—indeed, too powerful—because the method of its correction <note>original: "correctio"; the chemical process of tempering or purifying a substance to make it safe for medicinal use</note> was unknown to them.

### 71. *Separation of the Medicinal Liquid from the Moist Fire, after the separation of the Oil*

Regarding the liquid from which the balsam <note>In this context, "balsam" refers to the oily, resinous part of the substance</note> has been removed, that Medicine is separated with difficulty without the "killing" <note>The term "mortification" or "killing" in alchemy refers to neutralizing or changing the chemical state of a substance</note> of the moist Fire. Therefore, the moist Fire of the Salt must be killed by a contrary Fire, so that the required separation can be achieved, which you will accomplish as follows.

Take the liquid, consisting partly of dissolved worms and partly of the Fire of Salt <note>likely hydrochloric or another mineral acid</note>, and strain it through a filter so that it becomes clear and is freed from the skins of the worms, if any perhaps were not dissolved. If it passes through the filter with difficulty due to excessive thickness, pour in enough common water so that, having been made thinner, it penetrates the blotting paper more readily. Into this clear liquid, drip in the liquor of Salt of Tartar <note>Potassium carbonate</note> drop by drop; this will kill the sharp Fire of the Salt, so that from that Fire of Salt and from this Alkali Salt there arises a Salt of a middle nature <note>A neutral salt formed by the reaction of an acid and a base</note>, hardening into crystals and leaving the remaining part in a liquid form. This liquid will be either white or yellow, depending on the Fire of Salt that was used. This liquid, however, surpasses the hardened Salt in its strength, inasmuch as it contains the essence of the worms, which does not harden with the Salt but remains surviving in the liquid. Experience will teach these things more abundantly.

Furthermore, those things which before their preparation these...

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

The worms, not yet stripped of their poison, possessed certain qualities; they retain these same qualities after that very preparation, yet they are safe for use, whereas before they were dangerous. Moreover, the properties of these worms are as follows: they not only draw out all gouty humors <note>original: "Podagricos humores"; referring to the fluids believed to cause gout</note> from the body, but also, above all other medicines, they expel the stone <note>original: "Calculum"; kidney or bladder stones</note> from the kidneys and bladder, and furthermore they cure other such diseases which are plainly unknown and judged to be entirely incurable. Indeed, it completely removes that wandering arthritis <note>original: "volatilem illam arthritidem"</note> which roams through the limbs. But a question arises here:

### LXXII. *Whether any moist "Fire of Salt" is suitable for this work?*

I say indeed that this very work can be completed with any moist Fire <note>term: "Ignis Salis" (Fire of Salt); an alchemical term for concentrated mineral acids</note>, whether it be prepared from Vitriol <note>term: "Vitriolo"; sulfuric acid or iron sulfate</note>, or from common salt, or from saltpeter <note>term: "Sale petræ"; potassium nitrate</note>. However, I affirm that a great difference arises from these same salts. Concentrated Spirit of Nitre <note>term: "Spiritus Nitri"; nitric acid</note>, after its "mortification" <note>note: a process of neutralizing or killing the corrosive nature of the acid</note> with a fixed salt, congeals into saltpeter. Concentrated Spirit of Salt <note>term: "Spiritus Salis"; hydrochloric acid</note> goes into square crystals. The oil of Vitriol and Salt of Tartar <note>term: "Sal Tartari"; potassium carbonate</note> do the same. The salt existing in this operation from common salt excels the others in sweetness; that which proceeds from Vitriol produces nausea by its bitterness, and that which owes its origin to saltpeter is of a middle [nature].

The Spirit of Sal Ammoniac mostly precipitates the dissolved and corrected "worms" into a powder; once this is washed with common water and freed from its sharpness, it is used in dry form for medicinal purposes. The lye does not produce this effect, but always keeps the "worms" in a liquid form.

### LXXIII. *Question: Can many other useful things be learned from this dissolution of poisonous "worms"?*

Answer: Yes. For this operation not only teaches the good and bad properties of all "worms," but also clearly demonstrates that every living creature, of whatever kind—even humans themselves—when placed in the final struggle and sensing the approach of death, reveals and brings into view those internal motions of nature with which they were endowed during life.

### LXXIV. *A Method for Knowing the Internal Nature of Every Worm in the Earth, Fish in the Water, Bird in the Air, and even of Man Himself.*

By placing a beetle—whether one that lives in horse dung or another tinted with the color of copper—into *Aqua Fortis* <note>Latin: "Strong Water," the historical term for nitric acid, a highly corrosive mineral acid.</note> contained in a glass vessel, you will see that in the final necessity of death, it will not attempt to emerge from the *Aqua Fortis*. Instead, according to its innate property, it will strive to hide itself in the earth. Because it is unable to pierce the bottom of the glass, which is too hard, it struggles in vain to achieve its desire, attempting to pierce the bottom by striking it until it dies. From this, one may see what its final refuge was: namely, to escape imminent death by hiding itself in the earth.

A fly placed in *Aqua Fortis* will not seek the bottom, but will give every effort to emerge from the *Aqua Fortis*, because while living it always strives for the air, just as all winged creatures are accustomed to do. A fish, when reduced to such straits, strives to avoid danger by retreating to the bottom of the water.

In like manner, the nature of men cast into extreme difficulties comes into the open. A pious man, who has always thought of God during his life, will constantly cling to Him in his final struggle; and as he dies, he will take his refuge in Him, in whom he has always placed his hope.

<summary>Glauber describes how the "internal nature" of a creature is revealed during its death struggle, using experiments with nitric acid to show how beetles, flies, and humans return to their essential elements or spiritual foundations.</summary>

### LXXV. *The Preparation of Good Medicines from poisonous Vegetables, by means of concentrated Spirits of Salts.*

We have shown thus far that our "moist fires" <note>original: "Ignes humidos"; Glauber's term for concentrated acid spirits, likely hydrochloric or nitric acid, which "burn" like fire but are liquid</note> of Salts indeed destroy all things, but they do not burn anything away or drive it off as common fires are accustomed to do. Vegetables also bear witness that this is true; when placed into our moist fires, they are dissolved and turn into water. However, the Oil which they contained is separated and, occupying the highest place, floats upon the liquid so that it can be secreted from it, just as we indicated above regarding May-worms <note>original: "Vermibus Majalibus"; likely Meloe majalis, beetles used historically in medicine for their caustic oils</note>. In the same manner, the essence of a herb can be segregated by the Spirits of Salts, as we indicated in that same place. The Oils which are elicited from herbs and other Vegetables by this operation possess great and peculiar virtues, because they are excellently corrected and improved by the efficacy of the moist fires—a correction and improvement which distillations and pressings by no means provide for them.

### LXXVI. *The Correction of overly violent purgative substances through moist fires, so that their use may be safely permitted.*

By the stated method, the poisonous quality can be removed from **Scammony** <note>original: "Diagridio," a preparation of the resinous juice of Convolvulus scammonia</note>, **Hellebore**, **Spurge** <note>original: "Cataputiæ," likely Euphorbia lathyris</note>, **Gamboge Gum**, and other subjects that purge too vehemently, and can be converted into a sweeter and milder form.

### LXXVII. *The Correction of overly potent Diuretics, so they may be safely employed in the treatment of the Stone.*

Dissolve **Spanish Flies** <note>original: "Cantharides," beetles used in medicine but highly toxic</note>, **May-worms** <note>original: "Vermes Majales," likely oil beetles</note>, **Earthworms**, and **Woodlice** <note>original: "Millepedes"</note> using our concentrated fires, and follow those modes of operation which we have aptly prescribed: you will acquire an excellent and safe medicine, possessing the power to treat the **Stone** <note>original: "Calculo," referring to kidney or bladder stones</note> of the bladder and kidneys.

### LXXVIII. *The improvement of Narcotic and Sleep-inducing substances through our moist fires, so that they may exert their powers without danger.*

### LXXX. *Whether poisonous minerals, just like vegetables and animals, can be corrected by our Secret and moist Fire of Salt, and converted into healthy medicines?*

It must be known that not only poisonous animals and vegetables, but also all minerals teeming with poison can be improved, and their most potent poisons can be converted into the best medicines. For example:

### LXXXI. *How poisonous and volatile minerals are to be inverted by our moist fires, so that the volatile becomes fixed, and the poison becomes a medicine.*

<margin>Recipe <note>Take</note></margin> Take 1 part of white or red Arsenic, and pour over it two or three parts of the Fire concentrated from Niter <note>likely nitric acid or a similar caustic spirit derived from saltpeter</note>, which you shall draw off through an Alembic <note>a distilling apparatus</note> from a gourd-shaped vessel placed in a sand bath. Remove the remaining material from the glass and wash it with common water; once this is done, you will have a diaphoretic <term>diaphoretic: a medicine that induces sweating to expel toxins</term> Arsenic, which may be safely admitted into the body for use—though in a small dose, because it sometimes excites vomiting, especially when the Nitrous Fire has been somewhat weaker. However, so that vomiting of this kind may be prevented, the said Fire must be drawn off from the Arsenic through the Alembic two or three times, so that the poison may be better killed, and its volatility <note>original: "fugacitas," referring to the tendency of a substance to evaporate or escape during heating</note>

### LXXXII. *A method of changing volatile and easily-melted minerals through the moist Fires of Salts, so that they become fixed and difficult to melt.*

Let tin or zinc serve as our example; these are numbered among the metals of very easy fusion and are also considered to be very volatile. For it is commonly known that tin is liquefied by a very small fire, and even vanishes into smoke if it is kept in a continuous state of flux <note>meaning kept in a molten state</note>. If, however, it is roasted into ash by continuous motion, it becomes fixed, and it does not allow itself to be reduced to its former body by any violence of Fire, but is instead changed into glass.

By the same reasoning, we roast tin <note>original: "stannum"</note>, zinc, and other volatile metals <term>Metalla fugacia: metals that easily evaporate or change state when heated</term> with our moist fires, and we burn them into ashes that do not return to their original bodies. This occurs, of course, when we pour our fiery liquids upon them so that they grow hot together, or even when those metals are freed again from those liquids through the process of abstraction <note>distillation or removal of the solvent</note>. For these remain at the bottom like dead ashes, and they never allow themselves to be restored to their former bodies by any means.

**Note Well:** However, whoever knows how to apply the types of materials and powders that are accustomed to recalling such ashes back to their original and meltable bodies will not find their labor to be in vain. They shall obtain a metal of a much better and nobler nature than tin, the greater internal part of which consists of gold and silver.

### LXXXIII. *How fleeting Mercury is to be fixed, so that it may endure white-heat.*

Coagulate common mercury into a black ash using ordinary sulfur, and mix this same ash with concentrated oil of vitriol <note>original: "igne Vitrioli concentrato"; likely concentrated sulfuric acid</note>, or rather with that "fire" which is extracted from sulfur itself. Mix these until a thin mass results. Take about one or two lots <note>a "lot" or "loton" was a pre-metric unit of weight, roughly 15 grams</note> of this and wrap it in linens—either made of flax or cotton—layering them so that one always surrounds the other, with the mercury occupying the very center. Tie this ball or globe tightly and securely with thread, and surround it on all sides with fire, so that all those linens are heated to a glow and turned into burning coals. Remove this entire glowing mass so that it may cool. Strip away the burnt linens; once removed, you will find the mercury changed into a red powder. However, this powder has no entry into the metals <note>it cannot be used for transmutation</note>, nor does it offer anything of importance in medicine, because it has been converted into a "dead earth" by the combustion of our fire. I do not set down this coagulation here for the sake of making a profit, but only for the purpose of demonstrating the greatest powers of our "fires" through its operation.

### LXXXIV. *Another easier experiment demonstrating the possibility, through our Secret Fires of Salts, of making Mercury constant in fire, which the known and common fire is unable to achieve.*

Melt 1 part of common and yellow Sulfur in a crucible or an earthen pan. To this, once it is liquefied like oil, cast in 2 parts of common Mercury <note>Quick-silver</note>. Stir the material frequently with a spatula so that the Sulfur may take up the Mercury and turn with it into a black mass. To this mass, as much Sulfur must still be added as the weight of the entire mass existing in the pan. All things are to be melted together and mixed by stirring well for so long until they have taken on an ashen color. Into the mass while it is in flux, linen threads are to be dipped, such as are otherwise accustomed to be used for soaking barrels with a sulfurous odor so that they may be preserved from mold. Such threads, hung by a hooked iron wire, can be set on fire. By this burning, they are consumed; the Sulfur and a portion of the Mercury vanish into the air, but a portion—calcined <note>Reduced to powder by heat</note> and fixed by the flame of the Sulfur—remains adhering to the burnt linen. However, I cannot say with what powers this calcined Mercury might be endowed, as I have performed no other experiment with it. I have inserted this operation here solely for the sake of this matter: so that our...

Whoever desires to scrutinize the inner chambers of nature must investigate all things with a sharp mind and apply their hands to the labor. He passed it several times through a long pipe made of white metal plates or sheets, pierced with many small holes, and tried whatever other means he knew. ### LXXXIX. *By what means the concentrated Fire of common Salt may expel Antimony from a retort like Butter, and yield a material to be used with utility in Medicine and Alchemy.*

Upon the <term>Regulus of Antimony</term><note>The metallic form of antimony, separated from its ore.</note> crushed into a powder, pour the heavy, concentrated oil of common salt <note>original: "Salis communis oleum concentratum," likely referring to concentrated hydrochloric acid.</note>. ### XC. *The method of converting Mercury into a red and strongly purgative Medicine by the operation of Nitrous Fire.*

From one part of purified Mercury <note>original: "Mercurii"; quicksilver</note>, two or three parts of our Nitrous Fire are drawn off by distillation in a glass gourd <note>original: "cucurbitâ vitreâ"; a rounded distillation vessel</note>. This red Mercury, once freed from its saltiness by common water, becomes a powerful purgative. Its use is limited to a tiny dose—namely one, two, three, or at most four grains—and it is to be applied, not without curative effect, in the French Disease <note>term: French Disease; a common historical name for syphilis</note> and other detestable diseases of a similar nature.

### XCII. *On the Admirable Nature of the Magnet Attracting its Own Likeness.*

According to my simple and humble judgment, the red color of saltpeter <note>original: "Salis petræ"; potassium nitrate, often associated with "fiery" or "solar" properties in early chemistry</note> can be separated from its white body by the operation of no other thing better or more conveniently than by some magnet that touches it through kinship and likeness.

Let Gold and common quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; mercury</note> serve as an example; they are so combined with one another by a mutual bond of love and internal likeness that one attracts the other to itself. In handling mercury, if by chance some portion of it should fall to the ground, dispersing itself into thousands upon thousands of atoms <note>The author uses "atoms" here to mean the smallest possible physical droplets</note>, it can be collected by no better means than by the help of such a Magnet by which the dispersed particles are attracted—such as the Metals, and especially Gold, which is joined to it by the closest affinity. Therefore, when this mercury is widely dispersed with the earth and other filth and dross, from which it can hardly be distinguished because it is entirely covered by them, I sweep it up. I then place upon this filth a small piece of copper, silver, or gold; when this is rolled thoroughly through the dross, it attracts the mercury dispersed into infinite particles and atoms, and thus recovers it, having extracted it from those scraps. However, after the Metal has attracted enough Mercury and refuses to attract more,

The metal is then rolled again through the same dross, repeating the labor, to attract the remaining particles. This work is repeated until everything has been extracted, returning the mercury to you without any loss.

In exactly the same way, the innermost soul <note>The "soul" or "anima" refers to the essential, active chemical principle of a substance</note> of saltpeter <note>original: "Salepetra"</note>, which is widely dispersed throughout its entire body, could also be extracted—if only such a Magnet were known to us that possessed the greatest affinity for the Soul of Nitre.

I provide another, more evident comparison, so that the matter may become much clearer and more manifest, and thus be more easily understood.

### XCIII. *A clear and evident demonstration asserting that even the most hidden things can be made manifest and visible through their own Magnets.*

Let the admirable nature and property of the common Magnet <note>The lodestone or natural magnet</note> be properly considered, which nature has endowed with two plainly contrary powers: namely, one of attracting and the other of repelling. For with one side it draws iron <note>original: "ferrum"</note> to itself, and with the other it repels it. It performs this not only with its larger pieces, but even when it has been crushed into smaller fragments. For it always draws iron to itself with one side and pushes it away with the other, respecting both Poles in this operation—namely, the Northern and the Southern.

<summary>Glauber explains how mercury is recovered from metals and uses the physical properties of the magnet (attraction and repulsion) as an analogy for extracting the hidden "soul" of saltpeter.</summary>

# First Century

Hence it is only right that the Philosopher, the Chemist <note>original: "Chymicus"; referring to an alchemist or early chemical scientist</note>, and the Physician should have the natures and properties of things well-known and clearly perceived, if they desire to establish any good operation and produce good effects; for without this knowledge, one can by no means achieve their desired goal. Indeed, it is a great disgrace to wish to enjoy the title of Philosopher and yet remain an utter stranger to and ignorant of the properties of things. But the world wishes to be deceived <note>original: "mundus vult decipi"; a common Latin maxim suggesting people often prefer comfortable illusions over difficult truths</note>, being plainly unrefined in internal matters and taking account only of those things which fall under the external gaze. If a donkey were clothed in a lion's skin, it would strike fear into other animals, who would think it to be a lion, and that only because of its external garment—even though it would perform no deeds of a lion, nor indeed could it. A wise man, however, would soon notice the eminent length of the ears, and accordingly would not fear it any more than other irrational animals.

The same would happen if a lion were covered by a donkey's skin; the brute animals would not dread it, but would rather hold it in mockery, inasmuch as they would take it for a donkey. But a wise man would immediately recognize from the actions and gestures of the covered lion that the external skin was a mere casting-off, and that beneath it the true lion lay hidden.

So we see it is also arranged with the greatest part of men, who, in the manner of brutes,

Those who rest content with the external appearance of things judge poorly, and they plainly neglect and despise the internal natures and properties of matter. This is the sole reason why, until now, little or nothing has been achieved in either **Medicine** or **Alchemy**.

However, to return to my purpose, I shall demonstrate through clear examples and operations that the innermost and most hidden natures and properties of things are most evidently manifested and acquired through attracting and repelling magnets <note>Glauber uses "magnets" metaphorically to describe chemical affinities—the way certain substances naturally draw together or push apart</note>. For all things that exist have their friends and their enemies, which is proven by the following operation.

### XCIV. *An operation affirming that the internal and hidden properties of things can be manifested and acquired through attracting or repelling Magnets.*

Dissolve several lots <note>A "lot" is a historical unit of weight, roughly equivalent to half an ounce</note> of lead, entirely free of silver, in **Aqua Fortis** <note>Nitric acid; literally "strong water"</note>, and force the lead to seek the bottom by pouring in salt water; wash this with fresh water and dry it. This whiteness is nothing other than pure and proven silver, snatched from Saturn <note>The alchemical name for Lead</note> by the Sun <note>The alchemical name for Gold</note> through Sympathy, and thus rendered visible, which previously lay hidden within the lead in a spiritual and invisible state.

At this point, someone might ask: since so much silver lies hidden in any piece of lead, why are there none to be found who can extract it? I respond that there are indeed many who would extract great masses of silver from lead, if only they understood the art and were able to produce the effects. But because they are ignorant of the natures and properties of the Metals, and know not how to perform anything, they cannot see their wishes fulfilled. In this extraction, however, a twofold cause becomes known: namely, Sympathy and Antipathy. On account of a kindred friendship, Gold gathers the spiritual Silver from the lead to itself; and on account of an innate hatred which exists between it and the Salt, it repels the salt from itself. Therefore, in extracting the spiritual Silver from the lead, Gold has a helper assisting through a contrary operation, ensuring that so much more Silver is drawn out; for the Salt joined to the lead, because of the innate enmity by which it differs from Silver, drives the silver away from itself as if it were its own enemy.

Silver can always be melted out, even without the addition of any Gold, solely because of the enmity <note>original: "inimicitiam"; referring to chemical reactivity or repulsion</note> that exists between Silver and Salt, which causes the Silver to be driven out of the lead by the Salt, as if by its enemy; however, not as much Silver is brought forth as would correspond to half of that which is summoned when Gold is added.

For when Gold attracts and Salt expels, two actions occur simultaneously: one through Sympathy <note>The alchemical concept of "like attracting like"</note> and the other through Antipathy <note>The concept of substances repelling one another</note>, both seeking the same goal: that the hidden Silver and Gold may be extracted from the lead.

When two people agree so unanimously in pulling a carriage that one precedes it to pull and the other follows to push, it cannot happen otherwise than that it is moved forward. Thus, in this operation, Gold attracts and Salt pushes, yet both work in such a way that pure Silver is snatched away from old Saturn <note>A common alchemical name for the metal Lead</note>.

Let two small hundredweights <note>term: "centenarii"; a unit of weight used in assaying</note> of equal weight be evaporated in a cupel <note>term: "cupella"; a shallow, porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead</note>, and to one hundredweight let several pounds of pure Gold (adjusted to that same small weight) be added; more Silver will indeed come forth from that hundredweight by the aid of Gold than from the other to which no Gold was added. It is necessary for me to reveal these things, as they teach the manner in which the innermost, most hidden, and purest parts of things can be separated from the coarser parts. # First Century.

Since like associates with like and is repelled by its opposite, nothing remains for us but to discover what that "like" substance is, by which Saltpeter allows itself to be extracted.

To those of us who properly consider the origin of Saltpeter <note>original: "Salispetræ"; also known as potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder and fertilizers</note>, it is not unknown that it draws its beginning from the excrement of animals, and especially from horned cattle, such as cows and sheep. And since sheep and cows feed only upon herbs and grass growing in the fields, and these same Vegetables <note>original: "Vegetabilia"; referring here to all plant life</note> come forth from the Salt of the earth with the assistance of the sun's rays, it is clearer than the midday light that the hot Sun is the father of Saltpeter, the cold night is its mother, the earth is its nurse and progenitor, and Salt is its food, nourishment, and growth; this must be understood according to the Macrocosm <note>The "Great World" or the universe as a whole</note>. Furthermore, the Vegetables—namely all shrubs, herbs, and every grass which grows from the earth in the Macrocosm—can be compared more conveniently to nothing else than to the hair and fur of animals and humans in the Microcosm <note>The "Little World," referring to the human body as a reflection of the universe</note>, which grow from the earth of the Microcosm (the animal body), just as shrubs, herbs, and grass emerge by growing from the Macrocosmic earth. Hence, the hair, fur, nails, and horns of animals, likewise the feathers and claws of birds, as well as the teeth and scales of fish, correspond entirely to Saltpeter in their likeness; seeing as from these, together with other things of nature...

# First Century

hooves, feathers, and their scales are very similar to **Saltpeter** <note>original: "Salipetræ"; potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder and a major focus of early chemistry</note>, so much so that one part extracts the greatest powers and the innermost soul from the other in a **sympathetic manner** <note>original: "Sympathetico modo"; the belief that substances with similar natures naturally attract or influence one another</note>, and thus one reveals the other: For example.

When the skin, hair, nails, or hooves of a human or any animal, as well as the feathers of birds, are smeared or anointed with the **Spirit of Niter** <note>original: "Nitri Spiritu"; nitric acid</note>, they turn yellow like gold, and thus take on a golden color. It is asked, however, where does that color originate? Does it come from the hair itself, or truly from the Spirit of Niter? If that golden color arose from the hair itself, it would be absolutely necessary for it to manifest itself even if the hair were moistened with other sharp and strong waters <note>referring to other acids</note>, which nevertheless never happens, except when they are anointed with Spirit of Niter, or even with **Aqua fortis** <note>original: "Aqua forti"; literally "strong water," another term for nitric acid</note>, which contains the Spirit of Niter. It appears clearly from these things, therefore, that the superfluities of the **Microcosm** <note>original: "Microcosmi"; the human being viewed as a miniature map of the universe</note> have the greatest affinity with the superfluities of the **Macrocosm** <note>original: "Macrocosmi"; the great world or universe</note>, namely with herbs and grass. Hence it happens that one part withdraws from the other part its best virtues and powers and renders them visible, which previously lay hidden, invisible and impalpable, within their thick bodies.

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

### 95. *A method for extracting the Golden Soul from Nitre.*

If one were to pursue the most direct path of snatching the soul from **Nitre** <note>original: "Nitro"; likely potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder and alchemical experiments</note>, the crude Nitre must first be purified through distillation. Afterward, from this cleansed body, the purest part must be extracted by means of a suitable **magnet** <note>In alchemy, a "magnet" refers to a substance or reagent that has a natural affinity for another, drawing out its essence</note>. The thick dregs are to be removed, and this most pure soul must be brought to the highest level of perfection and dignity through concentration and **fixation** <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat</note>.

However, although I could set forth this method of extraction in clearer words in this place, it is not permitted for me to do so on account of those who are unworthy. This guidance is sufficient, indicating the manner in which it ought to be done: namely, through some magnet which, by a magnetic operation, attracts its like to itself. I am able to produce such a yellow **Soul of Nitre**—resembling **Gold**—at any time, and to apply it for use in the illnesses of my neighbor. But enough has been said of these things, concerning which I shall be more verbose in the following Centuries <note>Glauber often organized his writings into "Centuries," or groups of one hundred observations or recipes</note>.

### XCVI. *How the moist and cold Nitrous Fire is to be reduced, in order to restore it to its visible flame.*

To several ounces of your concentrated and moist Nitrous Fire <note>original: "Ignis Nitrosus"; likely referring to concentrated nitric acid or a similar corrosive spirit</note> in a glass vessel, gradually and drop by drop pour a sharp lye made from wood ashes, or preferably fixed Nitre <note>original: "Nitrum fixatum"; potassium carbonate or potash</note>. Continue this pouring until all noise, smoke, and boiling ceases, and the moist Fire itself becomes entirely still and dies. By this act, all corrosive power is taken away from that Fire, which by this operation has returned to its former nature, changed into such Saltpeter <note>original: "Salepetra"</note> as it was before it was converted into moist Fire. From this Saltpeter—now rendered purer and better through so many conversions and operations—a new moist Fire is to be drawn out again through distillation and concentration, far better and much stronger than the first. If, however, this second moist fire is again extinguished by the lye of fixed Nitre and changed once more into Saltpeter, and this is then converted into moist Fire by a new distillation and concentration, this fire itself will possess far greater powers. Indeed, by every mortification <note>the "killing" or neutralizing of a substance</note> and vivification <note>the "re-enlivening" or restoration of a substance's active state</note>, it becomes one degree stronger, nobler, and more effective; just as the Saltpeter itself, through such conversions and reductions, [increases] by individual degrees

### 97. *An operation showing the method—by the aid of Saltpeter <term>Salispetra: potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder and chemical refining</term> promoted to the highest degree of subtlety—of igniting and burning away the superfluous Sulfur of imperfect metals, just as common fire burns wood, so that besides a little fixed Salt and ash, nothing at all remains: just as in this combustion of impure metals, through our purest Saltpeter, nothing remains except that fixed Gold and Silver which lay hidden spiritually within the metal, and is left behind with the combustible dross.*

No one is ignorant of the fact that from any common Saltpeter and Sulfur, a gunpowder <term>pulvis pyrius: literally "fire powder"</term> can be made; however, it will not match the quality of that prepared from purified Saltpeter. The purer and more subtle the Saltpeter is, the better and stronger the gunpowder becomes. The same is to be understood regarding the other uses of Saltpeter.

It also escapes no one that common Saltpeter, by burning away common metals, reduces them to dross <note>original: "scorias"</note>, and washes Gold and Silver, leaving them pure. Concerning this "fiery washing," I have recorded many things in memory up to this point. However, that pure Saltpeter performs this washing better than common, and our "purest of the pure" performs it more effectively still, is far from being alien to the truth; experience will most clearly reveal this to anyone who is skilled in this matter—

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

...is about to perform the experiment. Truly, a small fire will not provide the same result that a larger one is permitted to provide, nor will this larger fire produce the effect that the greatest fire will achieve; this matter is so evident and manifest that no one can be found who would dare to deny it.

Take one part <term>Regulus of Antimony</term> <note>The metallic form of antimony, separated from its ore</note>, and four parts of pure tin; melt them together in a crucible and pour it out so that it may cool. This mass renders any iron or steel fusible <note>capable of being melted</note>. Therefore, when you are about to melt iron or steel, fill a crucible with one or the other of those same metals, place it in a <term>wind furnace</term> <note>A furnace designed to create a strong draft for high temperatures</note>, and leave it in the coals until all the material glows white-hot. 

Afterward, having removed the lid, cast into the same crucible a portion of the said mass (composed of the Regulus of Antimony and tin) equal to half the weight of the iron or steel placed in the crucible. Cover it with the lid, bury it in the coals, and urge the fire as strongly as you can, so that the added mass may bring about the flow of the iron or steel. Once everything is thoroughly liquefied, pour it out immediately, lest the tin, vanishing into smoke <note>original: "in fumum abiens"; referring to the oxidation or volatilization of the tin</note>, leave behind the melted iron, which would then return to its original hardness and refuse to be poured.

This material, consisting of the Regulus of Antimony, tin, and iron or steel, is so hard that a struck flint can strike sparks of fire from it.

Therefore, when you are about to attempt the danger of the aforementioned combustion, take a strong crucible made of potter's clay...

# First Century

Place a crucible made of clay and filled with saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note> upon glowing coals, so that the saltpeter may melt. Furthermore, take the rod previously formed from the casting of tin and iron; hold one end with tongs and heat the other end in the fire until it is white-hot, but not yet melting. Plunge this into the molten saltpeter so that the iron, along with the tin and the Regulus of Antimony <note>the metallic form of antimony</note>, catches fire like wood and passes away into smoke with a flame. For nearly all tin and iron are pure sulfur <note>in the alchemical sense, meaning the combustible principle</note>, and once consumed by the flame, they leave nothing but dross in the crucible. When this dross is washed with water, processed with lead in a cupel <note>a shallow porous vessel used in assaying</note>, and separated, it leaves behind the true gold and silver that were hidden within both metals.

For when the impure sulfur of iron and tin is burned away by the flame of such pure saltpeter, it is necessary that whatever good was within the metals must remain.

However, I do not set down this operation here to promise anyone mountains of gold. Not in the least. Indeed, I reveal these and similar labors for this purpose only: so that it may become known to everyone that saltpeter, when brought to the required purity, is accustomed to burning up imperfect metals just like wood. From this, it can be easily gathered that such pure saltpeter far excels the common variety in its strength. Truly, similar labors—burning away imperfect metals by means of saltpeter purified in the proper manner—yield pure gold and silver with profit

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

### XCIX. *A method for preparing crucibles of such a kind that they preserve molten metals for a long time, so that they can neither break nor be liquefied.*

**Take:** <note>The symbol ℞ stands for the Latin 'Recipe,' meaning 'Take.' The specific ingredients appear to have been listed on a previous page or are implied by the 'materials' mentioned below.</note>

Mix these materials and moisten them with common water so that they are turned into a mass. From this mass, you shall form small and large crucibles <term>crucibles: heat-resistant containers used for melting metals</term> by means of a crucible mold, driving them into the receptacle with a heavy hammer according to the method I described in the Fifth Part of my *Work on Furnaces*. Push the crucibles out of the mold and expose them to the air to be dried; once dried, they are fit for use and do not require any firing in a potter's kiln. If handled correctly, they can stand in the coals for a long time, they do not gape open with cracks, nor are they liquefied even in the most violent fire.

### *C. An Infallible Demonstration that all hidden things lie within Salt and Fire, or that by the help of the Sun and Salt all things are generated, arise, grow, and increase.*

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the foundational principles of natural philosophy or alchemy; this page transitions into a specific demonstration regarding the origins of life and growth. Catchword "Because" (Quia) connects to the following page.</meta>

# First Century

It is sufficiently and more than clear; it seemed worthwhile to me, at the conclusion of this treatise, to assert by a true and evident demonstration that all things are procreated, preserved, and increased by the Sun and Salt, as the primary and most noble creatures of God—provided, however, that the seeds of things are present, which require multiplication.

For although the Sun and Salt were even far more excellent and noble subjects than they currently are, they could not produce or generate even a tiny herb or a little worm—to say nothing of man—without a seed. If we possess the seed, it is granted to us by God to propagate it through the Sun and Salt. God has reserved the procreation of the seed itself for Himself alone. Therefore, the seed is sufficient for us; if we have it, we can propagate and increase it infinitely through the efficacy of the Sun and the powers of Salt, that universal nourishment of all things.

The universal medicine and nourishment of the peasantry, which they use for the preservation of their vineyards, fields, and pastures, is the dung of cattle and livestock. When this is applied to the roots of vines and trees, or cast into the fields and pastures, the plants <term>vegetabilia: specifically referring to the growing power of plants</term> draw to themselves their necessary nourishment, grow, and bring forth the fruits necessary for the nutrition and sustenance of men and beasts.

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues Johann Rudolf Glauber’s discussion on agricultural chemistry, specifically focusing on the production and application of artificial fertilizers as a superior alternative to animal manure.</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

Since this nourishment, extracted from animal dung by all vegetables <note>Glauber refers to plants as "vegetables" in the broad sense of all growing flora</note>, is nothing other than a **urinous salt** <note>original: "Sal urinosus"; likely referring to ammonium salts or nitrogenous compounds</note>, and since we know how to create such a urinous salt through the **benefit of Art** <note>original: "Artis beneficio"; referring to the "Art" of Alchemy or Chemistry</note> from common salt to be used for manuring and enriching fields in place of dung, we can certainly do without that dung if we are equipped with such a Salt. <margin>+</margin> This salt, having been **alkalized by fire** <note>original: "per Ignem Alcalisatus"; processed through high heat to become a caustic or alkaline substance</note>, spreads itself much more widely and lasts much longer in the earth for nourishing and manuring than animal dung does. Furthermore, it imparts a far sweeter taste and smell to all crops and fruits than dung itself; the truth of which will be experienced by anyone who puts it to the test. Indeed, what is of far greater importance: by the help of such salts, a most fragrant odor can be imparted to crops—and especially to tree fruits and grapes—if these salts are used in fields and gardens instead of dung, and if spices and other fragrant things are mixed with those same salts and added to the roots of the plants. The matter will become clearer by an example.

Turn over a sterile part of some field with a plow because it lacks dung, or even ground that is entirely sandy, and sprinkle as much of the said Salt as is sufficient upon it. Mix it into the earth itself by plowing and sow grain of any kind; it will spring up from the salty earth or sand, it will grow, and it will compensate the labor bestowed upon it with a hundredfold seed, just as well as—indeed, better than—if common dung had been used for that **manuring** <term>stercoratio: the process of fertilizing or enriching soil with manure or nutrients</term>.

<summary>Johann Rudolf Glauber argues that chemically prepared "urinous salts" are superior to animal manure for fertilizing crops, claiming they last longer in the soil and can even improve the flavor and scent of fruits.</summary>

From where, however, does that faculty of growing, and that power of increase and multiplication arise, if not from that unique Salt with which you have sown your sterile field? For from sterile sand and rain water alone, a seed is unable to take up for itself the nourishment and food required for growing and receiving increases. You will discover this to be true by an easy task, if you fill two wooden boxes with common sand, and mix into the sand of one of them one, two, or three pounds of that "inverted" Salt <note>original: "Salis... inversi" - likely referring to a chemically altered or "fixed" salt used as a fertilizer</note> (depending on whether that wooden box is larger or smaller), and leave the sand of the other box alone. 

If you then commit various seeds to both sands, and water them with rain water while they are exposed to the air, those seeds will indeed come forth from both by growing and will take on increases; but from the box which was sprinkled with salt, they will grow far more fully and perfectly, to such an extent that, continuing in their growth, they will arrive at perfect maturity. Meanwhile, the seeds of the other box will gradually perish and die.

From this it is evidently clear that the faculty of growing and taking on increase in all things proceeds from a unique Salt. If, therefore, peas, beans, oats, barley, winter rye, wheat, wine, and fruit of every kind grow and are increased by the benefit of Salt, it necessarily follows that Salt is the underlying substance and the universal nourishment. If you are unable to grasp these things with your "mutton-headed" wit <note>original: "vervecino tuo ingenio" - a derogatory Latin idiom comparing the reader's intelligence to that of a wether or ram</note>, remain a mutton and a fool along with your Farner <note>referring to Ehregott Daniel Colberg's opponent or a specific contemporary critic</note> and I.H.S., who determine...

<meta>This page continues Johann Rudolf Glauber's discussion on the practical applications of salts, transitioning from chemical theory to agricultural experiments.</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

Of the aforementioned salt <note>original: "Salis dicti"; likely referring to Glauber's "Sal Mirabile" or sodium sulfate</note>, which spreads itself more widely than manure and lasts longer. During this past spring season, I applied this inverted salt to certain half-dead vines which, planted in lean sand, had scarcely been able to grow to the length of a finger. Immediately beginning to turn green, they emerged with such vigor in their growth that a great increase could be noticed from day to day. 

And so, when on a certain day I was showing some friends in my laboratory the improvement of metals which can be achieved by the aid of common salt, and while they were marveling at the matter, I showed them those vines outside the laboratory, born from their half-dead trunks. Upon measuring several of the vine shoots, they found that in two or three and a half months, they had acquired a length of 6, 7, 10, and even 11 feet in growth, and were twice or thrice thicker than the trunks from which they had sprung. 

This change, or grand transmutation <note>Glauber uses the alchemical term "transmutatio" to describe the biological growth, suggesting the same vital force governs both plants and minerals</note> produced in the vines by the salt, would hold more wonder and utility than that of the metals, if only we did not look upon it with eyes greedy for gold, nor judge it with minds suffering from the cursed hunger for gold <note>original: "auri sacra fame"; a reference to Virgil’s Aeneid regarding the corrupting influence of greed</note>. And since this transmutation was observed in the month of July, and nearly three months still remained for the vines themselves to sprout and take on further increases, anyone can easily conjecture how much growth the wood of the vines can take on in the space of a single summer.

But let these things suffice concerning the inversion of common salt.

# An Admonition to the Benevolent Reader, or a Proposal Prescribing to No One,
### in which is shown how much benefit the common Fatherland can obtain from my inexpensive extraction of Gold and Silver from volatile minerals.

I have no doubt that the "Brothers of Ignorance," my enemies, driven by pure envy, will pursue this little work of mine with the greatest hatred and hold it in wicked derision—just as they have treated my previous writings until now. This is chiefly for the reason that I have not delivered everything expressly and clearly in all places, so that anyone might rightly perceive them. Therefore, I signify here to those not occupied by prejudice that I have done this very thing industriously <note>original: "de industriâ"; meaning intentionally or on purpose</note>, primarily for this reason: so that the most powerful foundations of the most noble art of Alchemy <term>Alchemy: the historical practice of chemistry aimed at transmuting metals and finding universal cures</term> may be concealed from the most unworthy slanderers and enemies of truth, and reserved for friends alone.

Indeed, I had firmly proposed to myself not to divulge this little work by printing it, but rather to give it to a few to be transcribed by hand, so that only true lovers of the Art might be made participants of the same.

However, because I lacked good scribes, I gave it to a Printer <term>Typographus: a master printer or typesetter</term> to be printed; nevertheless, I have omitted certain words here and there, lest all things—

And even if I had disclosed nothing else in this work but that single method of extracting abundant gold, silver, and copper with profit from poor ores—which cannot compensate for the costs of smelting—by using inexpensive waters <note>original: "aquas non fumptuofas"; likely referring to chemical solvents or acids like aqua fortis</note>, I would have bestowed a significant benefit upon our common Fatherland by this secret alone. This is because similar poor ores are found everywhere throughout almost all of Germany; they do indeed contain gold, silver, and copper, but they by no means allow these same metals to be taken from them profitably by the common fire of smelting. Among these ores, the first place is held by those containing "fleeting" <note>original: "fugax"; referring to volatile minerals that evaporate or disperse when heated</note> gold and silver. These ores lose the metals, which are driven off through smoke by the heat of a violent smelting fire; however, they are preserved, fixed, and rendered permanent by my attracting waters. Thus, even from the most fleeting ores and metallic veins, good gold and silver can be extracted with no small utility. Would not such works, therefore, bring the greatest advantage to our common Fatherland if they were established in more locations?

I have already pointed out such a vast advantage at great length in Part V of *The Prosperity of Germany* <note>original: "Prosperitatis Germaniae," Glauber's multi-volume treatise on national economic self-sufficiency through chemistry</note>, and the same...

# First Century

I have openly demonstrated the method of achieving this to several people in my **Laboratory** <note>original: "Laboratorio"; the author's workspace for chemical and alchemical experimentation</note> who were possessed by a desire to know it. Indeed, I am still occupied with demonstrating its truth to others, so that I may never cease serving our common **Fatherland** <note>original: "Patriæ"; likely referring to the author's home region or nation</note> with faithful service before the end of my life. I confess, however, that labors of this kind present no small amount of trouble to my old age. Therefore, I have firmly resolved to devote only this summer to such laborious demonstrations, and to spend the following time in a quiet life, committing the care of **Fire and Salt** <note>original: "Ignis & Salis"; a reference to the fundamental tools and materials of the chemist</note> to those who are younger and more robust. I have performed such labors to the best of my ability; let others do the same, so that they may bring even greater things to light. Truly, I have never been affected by weariness, nor have I ever spared any expense to maintain my laboratory over these last two years for the sake of the Fatherland. I have now made up my mind to put an end to these labors and expenses this summer, and I wished to indicate this to everyone at the present time.

In the meantime, however, for as long as the Divine will shall prolong my life, I will publish a few of my promised **Centuries** <note>original: "Centuriarum"; a common title for collections of one hundred observations or recipes</note> every six months. These will illustrate all my **Writings** <note>original: "Scripta"</note> published so far and bring them to completion <note>original: "ad umbilicum deducturas"; literally "to lead to the navel," a Latin idiom meaning to finish a book or project</note>, so that it will be possible for anyone—provided they are willing to set their hand to the work—to draw abundant utility from them.

<meta>This page continues Johann Rudolf Glauber's discourse on the practical application of his chemical inventions for the benefit of the state, specifically focusing on the establishment of laboratories for mineral extraction.</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

In order that my inventions may be of benefit to our common Fatherland in many places, I have considered it worth the effort to briefly point out my reflections on them, and to provide information on the method by which I believe this can be most conveniently accomplished.

First, I believe it is more than sufficiently well known that Princes and Great Lords are distracted by far different burdens and affairs than to be occupied here and there in the regions subject to them with the investigation and care of **metal mines** <note>original: "fodinarum Metallicarum"</note>. However, if they were to commit these cares to their ministers, these men would likely spend their time in banquets rather than in the laborious investigation of minerals and veins in the forests and mountains. As for the subjects themselves, being entirely unrefined and ignorant of such arts, it is easy for anyone to guess in what manner and with what outcome they would undertake such labors. These are the reasons why a matter of such great importance passes away without profit and is plainly neglected.

In my judgment, however small it may be, any Prince to whom many subjects are bound in due obedience would collect a great treasure of **Gold** <note>original: "Auri"</note> and **Silver** <note>original: "Argenti"</note> every year—and indeed without expense—if he would only establish a small **Laboratory** <note>original: "Laboratorium"</note>. In such a place, poor <note>In this context, "poor" refers to low-grade ores that contain only small amounts of precious metal.</note> minerals could be extracted with the proper waters <note>Glauber is likely referring to "acidic solvents" or "menstrua" used in the chemical leaching of metals.</note>. A faculty should be granted to any subjects who yearn for the knowledge of such arts to frequent such a laboratory and learn these skills there, added to the condition that each person [would provide a portion] of the extraction...

<summary>Johann Rudolf Glauber proposes that rulers establish state-sponsored laboratories to process low-grade ores, arguing that current mining efforts are neglected by distracted nobles and unskilled commoners.</summary>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on economic policy and mining, specifically focusing on the state's role in incentivizing the extraction of precious metals from low-grade ores.</meta>

# First Century

With the help of the aforementioned method, Gold, Silver, and Copper, smelted from poor <note>original: "pauperibus," referring to low-yield or low-grade ores</note> ores, would be brought into the Mint <note>original: "officinam Monetariam"</note> at the standard price at which they are valued everywhere, and would not be exported out of the region. By this reasoning, not only would the high Magistrate <note>The governing authority or local ruler</note> obtain a significant profit from the coinage without any expense or labor, but indeed every individual would devote the greatest effort to seeking out such poor ores, so that they might obtain Gold, Silver, and other Metals from those low-yield metallic veins, thus producing profit for themselves and their families.

If, however, a Prince or any Magistrate should refuse to permit his subjects the practice of such work—reserving it for himself alone—anyone can easily perceive that no one would devote effort to the investigation of such minerals. Instead, they would rather hide them, especially if the Magistrate, as sometimes happens, were to force his subjects into such labors against their will.

These things, according to my simple opinion—which prescribes nothing to anyone—would be the nearest way, without harm to any man but with the safety and assistance of many, to restore to our Fatherland the gold and silver coins of which foreigners have deprived it. But this will by no means come to pass unless the Magistrates themselves take the lead by establishing such a work, thereby inviting and inciting their subjects to undertake labors of this kind, which will provide vast Treasures for the whole Fatherland.

Secondly, each of the Princes and Nobles would most greatly promote the common welfare of their poor if they saw to it that the method of converting common salt <note>sodium chloride</note> by one hour’s heating <note>original: "candefactione"</note> was publicly demonstrated, and brought to such a point that it could be used in place of cow dung <note>original: "simi vaccini"</note> for the enrichment and improvement of thin vineyards, less fertile gardens, and other fields that are sluggish in producing crops. # Johann Rudolf Glauber

at one moment he extols it to the heavens with praises, and the next he despises it with the vilest insults; then again, he commends it as being of the better sort, and once more—more unstable than a tragic actor's buskin <note>original: "cothurno quovis inftabilior." For if it were free for every scoundrel, with feigned slanders and wicked lies...

# First Century.

If it were permitted for anyone to damage the reputation of an honest man at will, and afterwards hide themselves in some corner so that they could be found by no one, surely no honest man could protect the fame of his own name. Such a man would not even deserve to bear the name of a Christian if, by enduring such wicked deeds, he allowed the malice of such rascals to go unpunished. But a brief and calm patience usually delivers the innocent, with God providing the means by which the truth is brought into the open and lies are suppressed.

### A Second, Shorter Admonition.

The <term>Secrets</term><note>original: "Arcana"; refers to hidden alchemical or chemical mysteries</note> are unknown to no one, which I previously promised to reveal to Princes and Great Men in the Fifth Part of *The Prosperity of Germany* <note>original: "Prosperitatis Germaniae"; the title of one of the author's major published works</note>. For the sake of this matter, I spared no expense and no labor while I saw to the construction of a suitable <term>Laboratory</term><note>original: "Laboratorium"; a place for chemical work</note>, in which those three promised Secrets were publicly shown and demonstrated by me to all those who were desirous of such great things. However, since some perverse men have compensated my merits—which proceeded from a faithful and sincere soul—not with a due reward, but with injuries; I have seized the opportunity to refrain from the labors of such demonstrations in the future, and to spend my time in celebrating the honor of God, and [the benefit] of my neighbor

<summary>The author defends his reputation against anonymous slanderers and announces his decision to cease public alchemical demonstrations due to the ingratitude and harm caused by his detractors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the personal reflections and professional declarations of Johann Rudolf Glauber, likely following a discussion of his grievances with dishonest associates.</meta>

# Johann Rudolf Glauber

Lest honest men should pay the price for the crimes committed by the wicked, I have—God willing—resolved this winter to commit to paper not only those things which I have demonstrated in my **Laboratory** <note>original: "Laboratorio"; Glauber refers to his famous experimental facility in Amsterdam</note> for the grace and benefit of the Fatherland, but also those **secrets** <note>original: "arcana"; hidden knowledge or specialized chemical formulas</note> of greater importance which can confer the greatest benefit upon the entire human race. My goal is that in all places across the **terrestrial globe**, all mortals might be able to participate in and enjoy these things in these final times.

In the meantime, I kindly and earnestly ask that no one cause me trouble for having withheld and kept in my possession the **Key** to several obscure passages of this little work, which shall be brought to light, clear and transparent, in due time. For I have firmly resolved, and have hitherto proposed to myself, to communicate nothing further to any stranger. Instead, I shall reveal all my excellent inventions and **Secrets** <note>original: "Secreta"</note> of hitherto unheard-of benefit to the wicked and the good alike. To the good, this will provide an opportunity to persevere constantly in their goodness; but upon the heads of the wicked—if they do not return to a virtuous life—I shall gather burning coals <note>A biblical reference to Proverbs 25:22, suggesting that kindness or revelation to an enemy will make them feel the sting of their own shame</note>. Thus, I shall reveal to the whole world how much merit (let boasting be absent from my words) I have contributed to our common Fatherland.

Otherwise, I would have spent this entire coming winter on such demonstrations to serve the interests of the Fatherland, had I not been so basely deceived by wicked men—a fact which I felt absolutely compelled to make known.

<summary>Johann Rudolf Glauber announces his intention to publish his chemical secrets for the benefit of humanity while explaining his decision to withhold certain "keys" to the text due to past betrayals by dishonest people.</summary>

# First Century

and I have desired that no one should be ignorant of the reasons by which I have been driven to my opinion. Finally, as I have already done quite often, I testify again that those three Secrets <note>original: "Arcana"</note> promised in *The Prosperity of the Fatherland* <note>Referring to the author's earlier work, "Prosperitas Germaniae," which focused on national economic recovery through chemistry.</note> can be practiced with the highest profit wherever wood, salt, and metals present themselves. The truth of this matter I shall show and demonstrate most evidently in the very near future, if God shall grant me a longer life.

->*T H E  E N D.*<-
